### Native Department
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical)

>  The mode in which the Native Departmenthas been administered in the past hasnot been such as to inculcate habits ofindustry, and care, and exactitude in itsofficers. <i>(1909,NZ Parliamentary Debates,CXLVIII/1334</i>)

>  Hence the need of a permanent NativeDepartment, with the Governor at itshead, not responsible to the Assembly,but bound to supply it with everyinformation sought for. <i>(Jul 5, 1858,NZ Parliamentary Debates,1856-1858 / 581</i>)

>  Changes in the personnel of the NativeDepartment have been much more rapid thanMinisterial changes have hotherto provedin New Zealand. <i>(1858,AJHR,E-No. 1/11</i>)

>  He [the Governor] has no knowledge oftheir language, and hence is entirely inthe hands of the officials of the NativeDepartment. <i>(Aug 3, 1860,NZ Parliamentary Debates,1858-1860 / 190</i>)

>  The activities of the Native Department have reached the stage when the pakeha, who had been strenuous in his advocacy of the proper utilisation of native lands, was due to look for weaknesses ... <i>(Sep 17, 1932,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/20,1988 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1932 Sep 17 in 1988 Na To Hoa Aroha (Ngata-Buck Correspondence) 3/20 (Ngata)]

>  <b>RUTHERFORD</b> We talk of `taihoa' in connection with our Native Department. The real home of unadulterated`taihoa' is Italy ... <i>(1912,Impressions of a NZ Pastoralist on Tour,162</i>)

>  The activities of the Native Department have reached the stage when the pakeha, who had been strenuous in his advocacy of the proper utilisation of native lands, was due to look forweaknesses ... <i>Ibid.</i> 3/204 (Buck) I am pleased to hear that a change for the better has taken in the head office of the Native Department. The bureaucratic rule by civil servants who have no sympathy or knowledge of the native people that the department is supposed to administer ... <i>(Sep 17, 1932,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/20,1988 ed.</i>) [NOTES: Ngata]

>  This [Ruatoki] valley is Native land and is being farmed by the Maori owners with the assistance and supervision of the Native Department. It is only one of the many Native-land-development schemes scattered throughout New Zealand, ... <i>(1948,Our Own Country,49</i>)

### Native difficulty
 
 <i>n.</i> = Maori problem

>  <b>MANING</b> Talk to me of the `native difficulty' - pooh! I think it was in 1822 that an old friend of mine bought, at Kawhia, a woman who was just [212] going to be baked. <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,(xiv) 211</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAM MORGAN</b> Were this native difficulty settled, the country would go ahead with amazing stride, .... <i>(May 27, 1863,Journal,41,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>VOGEL (ed.)</b> native difficulty, native question <i>(1875,Official Handbook of NZ,65</i>)

>  My own impression with regard to the Native difficulties in the past is that there has scarcely been a difficulty - there has scarcely been a single war in this colony - in which the Europeans have not been more to blame than the Natives. <i>(Aug 16, 1881,NZ Parliamentary Debates,39/550</i>) [NOTES: John Ballance]

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> When Dr. F.F. Ormond purchased the Orangitirohia block on the north side of the river, not allthe Natives had been satisfactorily dealt with, and a `Native difficulty' of the first grade soon arose. <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,20</i>)

### native dock
 
 <i>n.</i> = Maori dock

### native dog
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McNAB</b> Q. Are their any quadrupeds in New Zealand, wild or tame? A. None but the native dog that resembles that of New Holland, tho' in New Zealand it is capable of being domesticated and they are trained to catch pigs. <i>(1908,Historical Records,1/536</i>) [NOTES: 1821 May Evidence of Ensign McCrae before Commissioner Bigge in 1908 McNab Historical Records 1/536]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... the native dogs are great obstacles to their [sc. poultry] increase for as their owners only feed them on vegetables it is natural that they will ... make a banquet on a fowl. <i>(May 10, 1839,Journal,2/131</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> The old native dog (kuri) seems to be extinct. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,135</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> Pigeons, nestors, parrots, rail, kiwi, swamp-fowl, water-fowl, owls, parson-birds, all these and more were eaten; while the native dogs and rats were held to be great dainties. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/151</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> Crozet described native dogs as looking like domesticated foxes, ... <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,167</i>)

>  <b>BEST</b> The native dog, now extinct, is said to have been introduced by old-time Polynesian voyagers from the isles of Polynesia. It was sometimes slain for food. The native rat was quite an important article of food. <i>(1925,Tuhoe: The Children of the Mist,11</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> ... I placed it [sc. soft whalebone] on the roof of a native house; but on the following morning I had the mortification to find that the rats and native dogs ... had eaten all the softer parts ... <i>Ibid.</i> 1/362 [The] native rat still lingers here and there [in the central North Island], although it has disappeared on the coast; the native dog has become a mongrel. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/50</i>)

>  And yet, strange to say, the numerous half-starved native dogs never think of destroying them. <i>(1841,Establishment of New Plymouth Settlement</i>) [NOTES: 1841 Establishment of New Plymouth Colony in 1940 Establishment of New Plymouth 76]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> What are called native dogs by the Middle Island shepherds are English animals, free, not wild. That the true native dog was introduced into New Zealand is proved by traditions, by the Maori term for dog being a true Polynesian word. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/22</i>)

>  <b>S. PERCY SMITH</b> At 2 p.m. we got into a pretty little clearing, where our dogs were driven in by some native dogs. It being such an unusual thing for native dogs to show so much pluck, we imagined there might be some Hauhaus about, so we left the camp. <i>(Feb 11, 1867,Taranaki Journal</i>) [NOTES: >1867 Feb 11 S. Percy Smith Taranaki Journal [=?Maori dog, i.e. a dog belonging to a Maori] in 1922 Cowan NZ Wars 2/533]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> It [Central Otago] was a silent land, voiceless save for ... the long-drawn, eerie howl of a wild native dog. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,118</i>)

### native duck
 
 <i>n.</i> -

>  There are eleven species of Native Ducks belonging to nine genera, all found elsewhere, except two--the little Flightless Duck of the Auckland Islands (genus <i>Nesonetta</i>) and the Blue Mountain Duck (<i>Hymenolaemus</i>). Among the most interesting of the non-endemic forms, are the Paradise Duck or Sheldrake (<i>Casarca variegata</i>), the Brown Duck (<i>Anas chlorotis</i>), the Shoveller or Spoonbill duck (<i>Rhynchaspis variegata</i>), and the Scaup or Black Teal (<i>Fuligula Novae-Zealandiae</i>). <i>(1889,Catalogue NZ Exhibition,117</i>) [NOTES: [Morris]]

### Native fashion
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LADY CAMPBELL</b> `That's native fashion; the Maoris know how to live, bless 'em!' <i>(1864,Martin Tobin,1/102</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> When clad in a shoulder mat, Native fashion, or when stripped to the waist with the haft of a tomahawk in his flax-cord belt, which supported his kilt-like piupiu ... <i>(1941,Numismatic History of NZ,37</i>)

### native flax
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Native flax. <i>(1785,Cook's Third Voyage</i>)

>  <b>CAPT. RAVEN</b> The flax grows here [in Dusky Sound] in great abundance, from which our people made fishing-lines and kellick-ropes. <i>(Nov 19, 1793,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1793 Nov. 19 Capt. Raven to Lieut-Govenor King in 1908 McNab Historical Records 1/179]

>  <b>SAVAGE</b> The dress of the natives consists in a mat - finely woven of the native flax, and its glossy appearance, with a fringe all round, renders it by no means an inelegant sort of mantle. <i>Ibid.</i> 56 Small baskets made of the green native flax. <i>(1807,Some Account of NZ,50</i>)

>  New Zealand must be always considered as the great emporium of the South Seas, from its local situation, its safe harbours, its navigable rivers, its fine timber for ship building, its rosin, native flax, etc., etc., specimens of which I intend sending to the Society by this conveyance. <i>(Sep 22, 1814,Letters & Journals of Samuel Marsden,133,1932 ed.</i>)

>  (And) <i>(Jan 1856,Edinburgh New Philosophical Journal</i>) [NOTES: Tancred Notes on the Natural History of ... Canterbury]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Flax, Native, n. The European flax is <i>Linum usitatissimum</i>, N.O. <i>Linea</i>. There is a species in Australia, <i>Linum marginale</i>, Cunn., N.O. <i>Linacea</i>, called Native Flax, In New Zealand, the Phormium is called <i>Native Flax</i>. See next word [<i>Flax, New Zealand</i>]. <i>(1898,Austral English,147</i>)

>  <b>BOYD</b> (And) <i>Phormium tenax</i> <i>(1900,Our Stolen Summer</i>)

>  (And) <i>Phormium tenax</i> <i>(1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology</i>)

>  (And) <i>Phormium tenax</i> <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology</i>)

>  <b>A.BATHGATE</b> ... we passed through an area where grew the most luxuriant native flax I have ever seen. <i>(1925,Random Recollections,19</i>)

>  <i>native flax n 2</i> : NEW ZEALAND FLAX <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1506</i>)

>  <b>GRUNDY</b> Our back yard rose steeply from the house to the fence line, where native flax had been planted as a wind break. <i>(1968,Who'd Marry a Doctor?,30</i>)

>  In all, New Zealand has over 15 800 ha of self-sown native flax, ... <i>(1974,NZ Agriculture,153</i>)

>  <b>ASKEW</b> The kits are made of native flax. They have two handles or loops to lift them by. They are the same in shape as those used by the carpenters, and some are as well made. <i>(1857,Voyage to Australia & NZ,347</i>)

>  <b>NEAVE</b> The [Otematata homestead] building [in 1858] consisted of the usual cob and clay, ... and thatched with cutty grass tied on with native flax which had been boiled to make it tough. <i>(1980,Land of Munros, The,65</i>) [NOTES: 1917 James Strachan Experiences (Hocken L.) in 1980 Neave The Land of Munros 65]

>  native flax n. plant yielding fibre used by aborigines for making fishing nets. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),621</i>)

>  native flax n. plant yielding fibre used by abos. for making fishing nets. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),14</i>)

### native forest
 
 <i>n.</i> [[NB: never native woods]]

>  <b>POLACK</b> <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/347</i>)

>  The southern portion of the county was originally covered with native forest, ... <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,274</i>)

>  The Island is much as it was but Wharekauri [his station] is returning to its native forest. <i>(Jan 24, 1915,Chudleigh Diary,414,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Here and there along the rim of the valley were patches of native forest. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/270</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> These exotic forest resources [insignis pine, Douglas fir, etc.] are being expanded to ... facilitate preservation of the native forests, which the early settlers wastefully reduced ... <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),11</i>)

>  <b>HAWES</b> ...suddenly.. he could see forest. Native, uncut. You can tell real native forest, it sort of sticks out like pop-up books, as though it's in 3D - or 4D - because it has another dimension from the flatness of other bush. <i>(2000,Dream of Nikau Jam, The,168</i>)

### native forget-me-not.
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) <i>Myosotis spathulata</i>

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Myosotis ... spathulata</i> ... A perennial forget-me-not, shaggy all over. ... [41] Native Forget-me-not. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,40</i>)

### native frog
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Leiopelma Archeyi</i>

>  According to information supplied ... the native frog is confined to the upper slopes of Mount Moehau, near the north end of the Hauraki Peninsula, and lives in the moss there so abundant.... A second species of New Zealand native frog was recently discovered on Stephen Island (see ...[TrNZI 1919 51/447-449]). <i>(Jan 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,3/308</i>) [NOTES: (Morgan)]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Native Frog - peketua, pepeke, <i>Leiopelma </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-12</i>)

>  <b>MCALLISTER</b> Halfway up, [we] found our first native frog, <i>Leiopelma Archeyi</i> - a timid brown and green creature less than 2 inches long. <i>(1976,Old Taranaki,183</i>) [NOTES: SIR GILBERT ARCHEY [1890-1974] was Director Auckland Institute & Museum to 1964]

### native grass
 
 <i>n.</i> [often in plural] (See [cf. English grass(es)](../E#english-grass(es)))

>  <b>YATE</b> The native grasses flourish throughout the year ... All English grasses flourish well; but the white clover never seeds; ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),75</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> The only plan will be to lease sheep and cattle runs, at a cheap rate for a long term of years, on much the same system as in New South Wales with the squatters; with this difference, that the rate ought to be even cheaper in this country, as there are scarcely any native grasses, and the whole face of the country is fern and swamp, which is improved each year by the introduction of stock, - ... <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,113</i>)

>  <b>EARP</b> `A few words on the native grasses will not be out of place. `The grasses of the Wairarapa ... are very numerous, and if cultivated, would be fully equal to English grasses: ... There is abundance of native rye-grass, and the sheep are very fond of a species of catch-grass ... ` <i>(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles),125</i>) [NOTES: 1853 Allom letter in 1853 Earp NZ]

>  <b>MACKIE</b> In his paddocks he has several of the english grasses, they are found to be very advantageous here, being hardier than the native grasses, they produce more herbage through the winter. <i>(May 2, 1853,Traveller under Concern,104,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> I may as well here correct an error, which I had been under ... - native grass cannot be mown. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,iv/47</i>) [NOTES: 1860 Feb 13 in 1863 Butler First Year]

>  Native grass is a good as oates and the open air in this climate keeps them [sc. horses] as harde as nails. <i>(Nov 1, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,203,1950 ed.</i>)

>  Mr. Holmes' sheep are running on native grass and the above increase has been obtained at one lambing. <i>(1914,History of North Otago,177,1978 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1865 Dec 1 in 1914 History of North Otago (1978)]

>  <b>BARKER</b> Nothing seems [25] so wonderful to me as the utter treelessness of the vast Canterbury plains; occasionally you pass a few Ti-ti palms (ordinarily called cabbage-trees), or a large prickly bush which goes by the name of `wild Irishman,' but for miles and miles you see nothing but ... undulating downs of yellow tussocks, the tall native grass. <i>(1865,Station Life in NZ,24</i>)

>  To facilitate the collection of such information, they prepared a printed list of such native grasses as appeared to them most valuable (33 in number), ... <i>Ibid.</i> 298 Several of the native-grasses, as Microloena stipoides, Danthonia semi-annularis, Agrostis oemula, etc., maintain their ground against, and unite with, several of the introduced kinds in the formation of natural pasture in many places in this province. <i>(1872,TrNZI,4/292</i>) [NOTES: Report on Grasses]

>  <b>BLAIR</b> In the olden times it was an accepted theory in Otago, that ... the milk of cows fed on native grass would not make butter; ... <i>(1884,Industries of NZ,59</i>)

>  In the Middle Island a large area is covered with native grasses. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,1</i>)

>  The extent of land in sown grasses was, in November, 1899, no less than 2,320,505 acres, while 2,042,789 acres were in tussock or native grass. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,11</i>)

>  Then on to see J.J.Fougere where I got a sod of buffalo grass and another sod of native grass ... <i>(Apr 17, 1909,Chudleigh Diary,447,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> In some districts the seeds of native grasses, such as danthonias, would be more suitable for sowing; ... <i>(1920,Australian & NZ Sheep Farming,122</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> Nearly all the ordinary commercial flocks in New Zealand started from the Merino, but change of pasture from Native to English grasses ... has driven the Merino back to the high country. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,50</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> In those days the rolling hill-tops were still native grass and virgin plume tussock, good natural pasturage long since ploughed under and replaced with imported grasses. <i>(1934,Children of the Poor,28,1949 ed.</i>)

>  In the light of these conditions, and bearing in mind that native grasses are the dominant crop on uncultivated areas, the dependence of New Zealand on a pasture economy can be readily understood. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,20</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> All the country was very rough, with scrub, fern and tutu mixed with native grass, especially long green almond grass, which made splendid cattle feed. <i>(1937,Thomas,101</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> But the native grass stands back,/ Uncouth and alone. <i>(1939,Selected Poems,85,1984 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1938-1939]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> From this onward [in Central Otago c1860] two native grasses, known to the early stockmen as barley grass and oat grass respectively from a chance resemblance to those two cereals, were predominant ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,119</i>)

>  The half-bred, three-quarter bred, and Romney sheep are more likely to overgraze areas of native grasses ... <i>(1941,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,22/22A</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> The early settlers of a century ago introduced to New Zealand various `English' grasses and clovers to supplement and later to replace the native grasses on the average-to-superior soil types. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,166,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>41</b> The floor and gently sloping sides were verdant with English and native grasses from the broad strath at our feet right up to where the cleared country narrowed and ran out in the higher ground of the bush range at the head. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,SUTHERLAND,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> Unless you walk over this country [Central Otago] you'll miss the native grasses, like danthonia and the blue tussock. To a Merino, one mouthful of this hard feed [13] is worth 10 of soft greenfeed. <i>(1982,Historic Sheep Stations NI,12</i>)

>  <b>BOYD</b> The Maori ran pigs in the swamp and grazed horses, sheep and cattle on the few rough patches of native grass on the side of the old track from Te Awa O Te Atua to Paki Paki and on the margins of the two creeks. <i>(1984,City of the Plains,4</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> [A.=Aust.; T.=Tasmania] -->[Only species marked N.Z. selected from the Morris entry and given below: A. = Aust.; T. = Tasmania.] -->Mr John Buchanan ... has prepared ... a `Manual of the Indigenous Grasses of New Zealand', which enumerates eighty species, many of them unnamed in English, and many of them common also to Australia and Tasmania. ...3 <i>(1898,Austral English,167</i>) [NOTES: Morris's list is incorporated into grass]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) indigenous grass species, principally danthonia and tussocks.

>  <b>BARKER</b> Tussocks, the tall native grass, has the colour and appearance of hay. <i>(1869,Station Life in NZ,25</i>) [NOTES: Station Life in NZ (1874)]

>  The pakihi seems to need further capital fertiliser after the fourth year, otherwise it starts reverting to yorkshire fog, lotus, native grass and fine-stemmed pakihi rushes. <i>(Mar 1987,NZ Dairy Exporter,26</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> And the carrying capacity of all the native grass ... <i>(1889,Colonial Couplets,2</i>)

>  In general hill country carrying native grasses is safe, but soem hill paddocks do produce the disease and these should be avoided. <i>(Mar 15, 1943,NZ Journal of Agriculture,146</i>)

>  The programme of development is for around 100 to 150 acres of native grass and more predominantly manuka scrub ... <i>(Nov 1963,NZ Farmer,16</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (historical) = Maori King

### Native land
 
 <i>n.</i> [often in plural]

>  Of the Native lands in this district, a very considerable portion has been leased to Europeans, but there still remains in the hands of the Maoris a valuable estate, ... <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,11</i>)

>  This [Ruatoki] valley is Native land and is being farmed by the Maori owners with the assistance and supervision of the Native Department. It is only one of the many Native-land-development schemes scattered throughout New Zealand, ... <i>(1948,Our Own Country,49</i>) [NOTES: from Korero, AEWS magazine]

>  The Government has it in its power to perpetrate the grossest favouritism by allowing one man to acquire native lands, while it prevents another. <i>(Apr 19, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,12</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> When he left school he got a job in the Native Lands Department - a swanky white- collar job like the Pakehas admire. <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,18</i>)

>  Take the case of the New Zealand Native Land Settlement Company. <i>(Aug 27, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  For the purpose of rendering any Nativeland or any land owned by Natives fit forsettlement, the Native Land SettlementBoard (formerly the Native Minister) maydecide to apply the provisions of section522 to that land. <i>(1934,AJHR,G-11/32</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) land owned by Maori.

>  <b>WILSON</b> There were two classes of offenders - those who leased land from the natives and paid them rent, and those who allowed their sheep and cattle to graze on native land and refused to pay rent for such grazing. <i>(1976,History of Hawke's Bay,212</i>)

>  [union platform] That native lands which benefit from the expenditure of special and general rates shall be placed upon the same footing as European lands so situated. <i>(Mar 9, 1907,Farmers' Union Advocate,4</i>)

>  With the idea of giving information to those requiring land, there will be published in this column full particulars of all Crown, public, and native lands thrown open for settlement. <i>(Aug 13, 1908,Weekly News,47</i>)

>  He exhibited a map showing the large tracts of native lands that were locked up in the Kawhia County. <i>(Jun 3, 1909,Weekly News,21</i>)

>  .. signs of a modest prosperity are forthcoming in the much-improved rate collection on European and native lands alike. <i>(May 3, 1945,NZ Farmer,6</i>)

### Native Land Court
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... an able an popular man, well known in the Native Land Courts ... <i>(1895,TrNZI,27/598</i>)

>  The Native Land Courts, in the eyes of the present generation of colonists, are chiefly interesting as the means of investigating Maori titles to land, and as the agency for peacefully transferring, by consent of the Maori proprietors, these lands to European owners. <i>(1876,TrNZI,8/422</i>) [NOTES: J.C. Firth]

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> A debt of this nature contracted some twelve generations ago was recently recognized by the Native land court. <i>(1941,Numismatic History of NZ,14</i>)

>  The Native Land Court of New Zealand (hereinafter called the court) shall be a Court of Record for the investigation of the titles of persons to Native lands and to hereditaments of which the Native owner shall have died intestate and for the other purposes hereinafter set forth. <i>(1865,NZ Statutes,29 Vict. 71/264</i>)

>  The Native Land Court of New Zealand (hereinafter called "the Court) shall be a Court of Record for the investigation of the titles of persons to Native land according to Native customs and usage, and for the other purposes hereinafter set forth. <i>(1873,NZ Statutes,37 Vict. 56/239</i>)

>  A judge of the Native Land Court may suspend an interpreter. <i>(1909,NZ Statutes,9 Edw. VII 15/256</i>)

>  There shall continue to be a Court of record called as heretofore the Maori Land Court. <i>(1931,NZ Statutes,22 Geo.V. 31/166</i>)

>  The cumbersome machinery of the Native Land Court had to be brought to bear on the several blocks... the Natives being fleeced by the lawyers, interpreters, Pakeha-Maoris, et <i>hoc genus omne</i>. on the one hand, and by the publicans on the other. <i>(Jan 23, 1890,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  Any European is considered more likely to do justice in the Native Land Court than a native expert, however learned in the lore of his race. <i>(Jun 25, 1904,NZ Observer,23</i>)

>  We cannot believe that either the Native Land Court or the Native Minister, any Native Minister, would not be particularly careful before approving an application. <i>(Jan 11, 1913,NZ Observer,3</i>)

>  Maori freehold land is essentially land which the Native (or since 1945 Maori) Land Court has converted from customary Maori tenure to English freehold tenure and has not been alienated by its Maori possessors. There are a good many specialised terms in this field such as ahiwhenua trust and putea trust. <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,2</i>)

>  The practice of the Native Lands Court lately is to endorse on the back of the memorial of ownership the names of all the persons having an interest in the block. <i>(Apr 5, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,12</i>)

>  English Law Courts are.. pantomimes in briskness compared with a Native Lands Court. <i>(Sep 3, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,21</i>)

>  A peculiar position was mentioned at a sitting of the Native Land Court at Greymouth. <i>(Feb 5, 1924,Otago Witness,1</i>)

>  Saw Rolleston who is holding a Native Land Court [at Christchurch]. <i>(Apr 22, 1868,Chudleigh Diary,220,1950 ed.</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a land snail, Powelliphanta traversi otaki

 <i>n.</i>

### Native Lands Department
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical)

 <i>n.</i>

### native lark
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [pihoihoi](../P#pihoihoi))

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Native Lark, dipping and rising. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,235,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> `Swampies' (swamp hens) and paradise and grey ducks were abundant in the waterlogged valley, while the native lark seemed ubiquitous. <i>(1947,Pioneer Recollections,5,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Pihoi (<i>Anthus N.Z </i>) is the so-called native `lark.' It is a ground pipit, and may often be seen fluttering and chirping about a bush road. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/224</i>)

>  <b>MANNERING</b> Stilts were common on the river - blackbill gulls, black-backed gulls, shags, dotterel, native larks (pipits). <i>(1943,Eighty Years,22</i>) [NOTES: [In Canterbury in the 1870's]]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> The New Zealand pipit, sometimes inaccurately called ground lark or native lark, is a local race (<i>Anthus novaeseelandiae </i>) of a widely distributed species ... <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/776</i>)

>  <i> Pipit Anthus novaeseelandiae </i> (GMELIN, 1789) ... <i> New Zealand Pipit A.n. novaeseelandiae </i> (GMELIN, 1789). <i> Auckland Islands Pipit A.n. aucklandicus </i> GRAY, 1862. <i> Antipodes Pipit A.n. steindachneri </i> REISCHEK, 1889. ... OTHER NAMES: <i> Pihoihoi, groundlark, native lark.</i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,270</i>)

>  The eggs of the native lark.. are so marked as to be well concealed in the tussock and fern where they are laid. <i>(Dec 11, 1890,Otago Witness,41</i>)

>  The native lark is a pipit. <i>(Mar 18, 1924,Otago Witness,70</i>)

### native laurel
 
 <i>n.</i> (mainly NI) Laurelia novae-zelandiae, =pukatea.



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Griselinia littoralis </i> (unless the ref. here is to karaka???), =broadleaf. [(?early Otago occas.)]

>  <b>PYKE</b> The native laurel (the `broadleaf` of the colonists) ... <i>(1874,George Washington Pratt,42</i>)

### native lease
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) land owned by Maori and leased for farming.

>  <b>WESTMACOTT</b> He has some 6,900 acres of native lease, and only needed the capital to bring it all into profit. <i>(1977,After-Breakfast Cigar, The,134</i>)

### native lilac
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Quintinia serrata</i>, =tawhewheo, =kumarahou.

>  <b>FEATON</b> QUINTINIA SERRATA (<i> A.Cunn </i>.) ... The `NATIVE LILAC'.- The typical form of this tree is confined to the kauri district, about Auckland, where it varies from a shrub with lilac-coloured flowers, to a tree forty feet high ... <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,131</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> =karaka [unlikely].

>  [Native name] Karaka .. [Settlers' name] Native lilac .. [Family] Saxifrageae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,122</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ]

### native logging
 
 <i>n.</i> logging of native timber

>  We have stopped native logging six years earlier than was ever intended. <i>(Sep 18, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

### Native magistrate
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There was unveiled.. a monument in memory of Reihana Kiriwi, who was for many years native magistrate in this district. <i>(Apr 16, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  He was a native magistrate. <i>(Nov 5, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,22</i>)

### native mahogany
 
 <i>n.</i> kohekohe

>  Native mahogany (kohekohe) trees stand proudly on the hillsides, lucky survivors of a mishap when an early traction engine fell into the sea when being moved to another site. This brought an end to the New Zealand Mahogany Company, which was based in the harbour. <i>(Sep 20, 2005,Dominion Post,A9</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [also Maori mat](../A#also-maori-mat),[mat](../M#mat))

Williams Early Journals 217 Some few of the leading men had a mantle of scarlet cloth trimmed with dogs' hair, other had splendid native mats: ... <i>(Jan 7, 1827,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,36,1961 ed.</i>)

### Native Minister
 
 <i>n.</i> [A real doozy this one, as the Yanks say. Lots of confusion, popular use masquerading as official and confused official use; am working my way through but have to go back to Turnbull where a helpful young lady produced as I was leaving the NZ Almanacks which seem to be the ONLY source of official titles. Will chase them up further. People in Hansard use very loose terminology and there is no official listing of Ministers in Hansard. The following is my best effort from the Wellington Almanack (and others): 1822-1850 No record. 1851 Commissioner for acquiring the Cession of Lands from the Natives: - Donald McLean. 1851 Native Secretary H. Tacy Kemp GBP200 per annum [in addition to above]. 1852 Native Secretary [from Estimates]. 1853 Native Secretary Native Secretary's Department [from Estimates]. 1855 Native Secretary's Department [from Estimates]. 1857 Native Secretary - D. McLean. 1858 AJHR E-No.1/17 Donald McLean, Native Secretary, writes from Native Secretary's Office, 28 June 1858. 1860 Native Secretary's Department. 1861 Minister for Native Affairs - Honourable C.W. Richmond Native Secretary's Office. 1862 Minister for Native Affairs - F.D. Bell (Acting) Native Secretary's Department OR Native Department. 1863 Native Minister - F.D. Bell Native Land Purchase Department - Crown Commissioner .. D.McLean Native Secretary's Department - Native Secretary D. McLean NOTE; This is the first time the big three Ministers are joined by a new portfolio. 1864 Minister for Native Affairs - Honourable William Fox Native Secretary's Department. 1866 Native Minister - Honorable A.H. Russell Native Department - Minister for Native Affairs - Honorable A.H. Russell. 1867 Native Minister - Honorable J.C. Richmond Native Department - Minister for Native Affairs. 1868 Native Department - Under Secretary W. Rolleston. 1870 Ministry has now become Cabinet Donald McLean, Minister for Colonial Defence and Native Affairs. 1873 Honorable Donald McLean, Native Minister. 1878 Native Minister & Minister of Justice. 1879 Native and Defence Office Native Minister. 1880 Honorable John Boyce, Minister for Native Affairs Native Office. 1885 Native and Defence Minister Native Office. 1888 Minister of Public Works and Native Affairs. I gave up about here. Basically, you can take your pick.]

>  There was nothing in the Bill itself orin the speech of the Native Minister inintroducing it, to show what area wouldbe affected by this legislation. <i>(1873,NZ Parliamentary Debates,XIV/351</i>)

>  The Prime Minister is the NativeMinister, and he is looked upon by theNative people as the Great White Chief,which undoubtedly he is. <i>(1926,NZ Parliamentary Debates,CCXI/289</i>)

>  Why should there not be a permanentNative Minister as His Excellency'sAdviser on Native affairs? <i>(Jul 5, 1858,NZ Parliamentary Debates,1856-1858 / 581</i>)

>  Ministers understand that the system lastdescribed, is that actually in operationunder His Majesty's Memorandum of 28August, 1856.. although in consequence,mainly, of the want hitherto of a NativeMinister, it has scarcely yet been brougheffectively into play. <i>(1858,AJHR,E-No. 1/10</i>)

>  Old colonists, who recollected how Mr.Clarke and other Native Ministers hadbeen got rid of, know well how much suchsort of stability was worth. <i>(Jul 5, 1858,NZ Parliamentary Debates,1856-58 ? 582</i>)

>  He had listened attentively to thestatement of the Native Minister; itopened no new ground. <i>(Aug 3, 1860,NZ Parliamentary Debates,1858-1860 / 187</i>)

>  Dismissing, then, these proposals, I turnto the policy of the present Governmentas indicated by my honourable friend theNative Minister. <i>(Aug 10, 1860,NZ Parliamentary Debates,1858-1860 / 276</i>)

>  I can.. even sympathize with resistancewhich has been made by the NativeMinister. <i>(Sep 25, 1860,NZ Parliamentary Debates,1858-1860 / 578</i>)

>  A reference to [the documents] will showthat the conduct of Native affairs wasundoubtedly reserved by the Governor.<i> Ibid </i> He was of the opinion that Ministersshould have no control over Nativeaffairs. <i>(Jun 21, 1858,NZ Parliamentary Debates,1856-1858 / 545</i>)

>  I cannot address myself to theconsideration of measures.. withoutlooking to.. the department of theExecutive Government which has themanagement of Native affairs. <i>(Aug 10, 1860,NZ Parliamentary Debates,1858-1860 / 275</i>)

>  I desire to thank the Minister for NativeAffairs for his very able statement. <i>(Aug 3, 1860,NZ Parliamentary Debates,1858-1860 / 187</i>)

>  Before the Native Minister removed the A.C.'s there was every chance of starting a butter and cheese factory at Pungarehu. <i>(Mar 28, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  <b>MANING</b> ... I must confess I don't know any more about the right way to tell a story, than a native minister knows how to `come' a war-dance. <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,(i)8</i>)

>  Papers written in 1874 by Sir William Fox, M.H.R., and Sir Donald McLean, then Native Minister, state that at what time the discovery was made by the Maoris ... are matters which are lost in obscurity ... <i>Ibid.</i> 275 The latest feature in Native affairs is the change which has come over Tawhiao, the so-called Maori King. ... He has ... quite recently ... accepted a pension from the present Native Minister. ... it is hoped that it will lead to the end of the `King movement'. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,3</i>)

>  ... the hectic career of the Native Minister should be stayed ... <i>(Sep 17, 1932,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/20</i>) [NOTES: Ngata. 1932 Sep 17 in 1988 Na To Hoa Aroha (Ngata-Buck Correspondence)]

>  The police will not serve it [sc. an information] till they receive direct instructions from the Native Minister. <i>(Mar 15, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  For the purpose of rendering any Nativeland or any land owned by Natives fit forsettlement, the Native Land SettlementBoard (formerly the Native Minister) maydecide to apply the provisions of section522 to that land. <i>(1934,AJHR,G-11/32</i>)

>  <b>IRVINE & ALPERS</b> When the Grey Government went out ofoffice, Mr. John Bryce succeeded Mr.Sheehan as Native Minister. <i>(1902,Progress of NZ in the 20th Century,303</i>)

### native mistletoe
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Five native mistletoe species are among more than 400 plants species identified as being at risk of extinction. <i>(May 2004,Footnotes,No. 21/8</i>)

>  The [Greytown] motor camp is one of the few places you can still find native mistletoe. <i>(Dec 17, 2003,Capital Times,6</i>)

>  The magnificent beech forests of the South Island, now aflame with the scarlet flower of the native mistletoe. <i>(Jan 26, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,19</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> It is placed on the lateral fork of totara, supported underneath by an epiphytic growth of native mistletoe (<i>Loranthus micranthus</i>). <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/233</i>)

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> <i>V[iscum] Lindsayi </i>[Oliver] is one of the `Native Mistletoes' of the settler: an extremely insignificant one ... when compared with the handsome Otago species of <i>Loranthus,</i> whose showy scarlet flowers alone are sometimes ... two inches long ... <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,52</i>)

>  One [new plant] is a native mistletoe with large red flowers.. <i>(Feb 5, 1924,Otago Witness,6</i>)

>  On the whole the beech forest is dark and gloomy, but here and there it may be lighted up by a tuft of scarlet or yellow native mistletoe [fn Species of <i>Loranthus.</i>], or in spring by the gay garlands of white clematis; while in the more open places grows the beautiful ribbon-wood [fn <i>Gaya ribifolia</i>, slightly different from the more western <i>Gaya lyallii</i>.], with a profusion of white flowers resembling pear-blossom. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,186</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> `From this point back to Moawhango the track, for some miles, leads through a lovely piece of bush where again native mistletoe rautawhiri [=P. tenuifolium] and other lovely shrubs were in bloom.' <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,49</i>) [NOTES: c1880s an anonymous tourist in 1959 L. Masters Tales of the Mails 49]

>  New Zealand has eight native species, seven of them endemic and one found also on Norfolk Island. A ninth species, also endemic, was last seen in the 1950s and is presumed extinct. <i>(Sep 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 5/80</i>)

### native olive
 
 <i>n.</i> A name for one of the plants of the olearia group. (See [olive](../O#olive))

### native oven
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [Maori oven](../M#maori-oven),[umu](../U#umu))

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> A'ngi; Name of a certain tree; a native oven; ... <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,134</i>) [NOTES: [|prec.= acute, '= stress]]

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> Coppre [kopa] Mourie, or the Native Ovens are described I rather think in Captain Cookes Voyages. They are done this way. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),44,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The only method of cooking formerly in use [by the New Zealanders] was by the kapa [a's-acute] or angi maori, the native oven, already described. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/399</i>)

>  <b>BIDWILL</b> ... and when I visited it, I saw all the native ovens (copper mowries, according to English pronounciation) in which the cooking had been performed, and a portion of the entrails, etc., were strewed about. <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,30,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SELWYN</b> A small native village is here [at Ohinemutu], with the usual appurtenances of a native steam kitchen at the hot springs; namely, hot plates, made of large slabs of stone, laid over boiling water ... steam hanghis, or native ovens, always in readiness ... A native swing completes the equipment of this fashionable watering place ... <i>(1845,New Zealand (Selwyn),Part II (Church in the Colonies VII) 32</i>) [NOTES: 1843 Oct 30 Selwyn Journal in 1845 NZ Part II (Church in the Colonies VII)]

>  <b>ANGAS</b> On one occasion, when on a visit to one of the missionaries at Waimate, a fine gander attracted his [sc. Waka Nene's] attention, and he secretly ordered it to be seized, and prepared for his dinner in a native oven; ... <i>Ibid.</i> 2/125 ... Newman [was] busily engaged in cooking ... the pig, in a native oven in the ground between hot stones. <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/298</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> The friendly natives ... were busy ... cleaning their arms, singing `hakas', and making speeches, while waiting till the old women opened the native ovens, which, well filled with pork and kumeras, sent up an appetising steam in all directions. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1849 Power Sketches in NZ 29 Aug 24th [1846].-]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> KAURU, the root of the Ti or Whanake after it has been baked in the native oven. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,308</i>) [NOTES: Vocab]

>  <b>MACKIE</b> Two women were preparing a native oven as we passed and we stopped to watch the process. <i>(Apr 5, 1853,Traveller under Concern,97,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> Two long lines of native ovens mark the spot where the bodies were cooked; and a smaller oven with a wreath around its edge ... <i>(1853,Auckland,145</i>)

>  <b>MOSER</b> I am regaled with some potatoes and a bit of fish, admirably cooked in a native oven; ... <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,6</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Hapii, n. native oven or cooking pit; haangi. <i>Ibid.</i> 59 kopa ... 2. ... native oven. <i>(1871,Dictionary of the Maori Language,19,3rd ed. ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Hangi, n. native oven. <i>Ibid.</i> 21 Hapi, n. native oven or cooking pit; haangi. <i>(1892,Dictionary of the Maori Language,20</i>)

>  It ... is generally known among European settlers as the `native oven', though the term `steaming-pit` would be a more exact description. <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/23</i>)

>  <b>GORST</b> <i>(1908,New Zealand Revisited,108</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Hangi, n. 1. Native oven, consisting of a circular hole in the ground, in which the food was cooked by heated stones. 2. Contents of the oven. <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,34</i>)

### native owl
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment)

>  mopoke, morepork The aboriginal name for Australian bird, adopted in New Zealand for the native owl. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),265</i>)

### native parrot
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> indigenous parrot, the kaka [aka kaka]

>  One of their pastimes was to find a nest of native parrots and to put a large cage over it, with netting so that the mother bird could feed the young through the holes. <i>(Apr 2004,NZ Memories,47/52</i>)

>  <b>1978</b> The first year after it had been completed the roof was entirely torn off by an invading army of kakas and native parrots, which came there in thousands just as the paraquets have done in more recent times. <i>(1914,History of North Otago,32</i>) [NOTES: 1894 Oamaru Mail in 1914 History of North Otago (1978) 32]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Kaka, ... Nestor meridionalis, native parrot. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,96</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Through the forests the outlaws and the native parrots, the red-plumed kakas, had swept in varying degrees of magnificence. <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,31</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [kohia](../K#kohia))

### native pepper
 
 <i>n.</i>

### native pepper tree
 
 <i>n.</i> Kawakawa

>  <b>DAWSON</b> Kawakawa is sometimes called `native pepper tree' because of its hot tasting leaves, and it is in fact related to the true pepper plant of Indonesia. ... Horopito (<i>Pseudowintera axillaris</i>) with its dark green shiny leaves has also been termed `pepper tree' for the same reason, but it is not in fact related to the kawakawa. <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,103</i>)
