### Native Protector
 
 <i>n.</i> an official appointed to look after the interests of Maori; a Protector of Aborigines.

>  It may be said that they are themselves possessed of sufficient funds for this purpose, in the tenth of land reserved for them; but it is worse than useless - it is cruel to talk of such a reserve, while at present much of it is not available, and very probably may not be so for a considerable time, in consequence of the difficult and therefore protracted nature of the surveys; and also from the absence of the Native Protector, as he is officially designated, who .. might by his presence here help to facilitate arrangements on behalf of the natives. <i>(Jul 31, 1841,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)

>  Supposing this intended with a view of giving full effect to the system of amalgamation, there should be no exceptional law for natives within the municipalities, Native Protectors, for example, are an exceptional class of officers, whose very appointment suggests the idea of a natural hostility between the colonists and the Natives; ... letters addressed to `Squire Clarke', the chief Protector of the Aborigines under Captain Hobson ... <i>(1949,Selected Documents,15A</i>) [NOTES: >1846 Jan 20 E.G. Wakefield to Gladstone in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 15A]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> At Kapiti, ... [Fitzroy] told the natives to be guided by `their true friends the Missionaries, the Native Protectors, and the `Government Officers,' - thus virtually advising suspicion ... towards the settlers. <i>Ibid.</i> 80 Among other useful ameliorations, [Grey] abolished the office of [81] Native Protector; ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,76</i>)

>  The natives who are at present at work on the diggings are fast acquiring a knowledge of gold mining , and some of them are doing exceedingly well. I [Mr. Mackay, jnr.] have been requested by several of them to act as native protector. ... as the natives become more numerous, it will be essential to appoint a person for this purpose. <i>(Jul 4, 1857,Lyttelton Times,5</i>)

>  After receiving a good bullying from the Native Protector.. for swearing to my own goods, the native was fined double the value of the hat. <i>(Sep 17, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

### Native question
 
 <i>n.</i> = Maori problem (See [Native difficulty](../N#native-difficulty),[Native problem](../N#native-problem))

>  We are all happy in a prospect of a speedy settlement of the native question. <i>(Nov 23, 1842,Letters from New Plymouth,70</i>) [NOTES: (1842 Nov 23) 1843 (1968) Letters from New Plymouth 70]

>  <b>WOOD</b> The Company's agent promises roads, and the settlement of the native question, ... <i>(1843,Twelve Months in Wellington,16</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAM MORGAN</b> The native question is still absorbing the attention of the people of this colony. <i>(Jul 5, 1863,Journal,42,1963 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1863 July 5 Journal William Morgan (1963) 42]

>  <b>RUTHERFORD</b> What is called the Native Question, presents greater difficulties. <i>(1946,Selected Documents,18</i>) [NOTES: 1846 May 26 Gladstone to G. Grey in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 18]

>  <b>RUTHERFORD</b> 1 The tenor of this [Ministers' memorandum on Native Affairs] would lead a person unacquainted with the past history and present condition of this Colony to suppose that now, forthe first time, the difficulties of the native question have been properly appreciated and successfully grappled with; <i>(1946,Selected Documents,105</i>) [NOTES: Oct 13 Memorandum by Native Secretary in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 105.]

>  The Native Question is growing into a subject of anxious consideration. <i>(Jul 4, 1857,Lyttelton Times,5</i>)

>  A frank ... discussion of their supposed grievances ... might bring the whole affair to an end, and place the country in a much better position as regards the `native question' than it has ever hitherto obtained. <i>(1860,Maori King Movement,72</i>)

>  We spent Three Million Pounds, and more,/ Upon the `Native Question'./ <i>(1868,Punch or the Wellington Charivari,16</i>)

>  <b>MOSER</b> To attempt to solve the difficulty of the Native question has not been my object. <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,69</i>)

>  The Native question now has lost much of the importance it once possessed. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,275</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> While the smugglers erased the customs along the coast, the native question was becoming progressively more complicated. <i>Ibid.</i> 149 ... a struggle for control of the land, and the land question involved the native question and the system of precarious military alliances established by Fitzroy. <i>(1958,Early Victorian NZ,82</i>)

>  <b>McNEISH</b> `It's the native question, you see, and this stupid new Governor.' <i>(1970,Mackenzie,23</i>)

### native rat
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [kiore](../K#kiore))

>  The whole lot were, in all probability native rats, though they are supposed to have become extinct in the Wairarapa upwards of twenty years ago. <i>(Jul 17, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  It was plain from earlier records that the native rat existed in hordes in the early days of the Maori in New Zealand. <i>(Apr 10, 1934,Feilding Star,7</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> kiore (See [also Maori rat](../A#also-maori-rat))

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> The Native Rat was more like a field Mouse and was considered a Luxury in old times, but the Rat from Norway or England came and is called Rah Parkieah or Stranger rat. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),53,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... formerly before the introduction of pigs the native rat was much prized and sought after all the rat runs were known <i>(May 10, 1939,Journal,2/130</i>)

>  <b>BEST</b> Dieffenbach got a Native rat. <i>(Apr 9, 1841,Journal,286,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> [The] native rat still lingers here and there [in the central North Island], although it has disappeared on the coast; the native dog has become a mongrel; ducks, teals, the red-billed porphyrio or pukeko, a large bittern, a tern, two kinds of gulls, cormorants ... are found here. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/362</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> The native rat (Mus rattus) is the only known indigenous land quadruped; but ... I was unable to find any. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,135</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> Pigeons, nestors, parrots, rail, kiwi, swamp-fowl, water-fowl, owls, parson-birds, all these and more were eaten; while the native dogs and rats were held to be great dainties. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/151</i>)

>  The native rat, which the Maoris named Kiore, was very different to the Norway rat, and strictly herbivorous. <i>(1914,History of North Otago,4</i>) [NOTES: 1906 W.H.S.Roberts in 1914 History of North Otago (1978) 4]

>  The kiore maori, or native rat, is said in Maori tradition to have been imported ... from Polynesia ... Many persons stoutly maintain that the old Maori rat is still with us ... <i>(1909,TrNZI,41/242</i>)

>  <b>SHAND</b> Native Rats, called Kiore, were common to the [Chatham] island: but it is believed they were not eaten by the Morioris .. <i>(1911,Moriori People of the Chatham Islands, The,6</i>)

>  <b>BEST</b> The native dog, now extinct, is said to have been introduced by old-time Polynesian voyagers from the isles of Polynesia. It was sometimes slain for food. The native rat was quite an important article of food. <i>(1925,Tuhoe: The Children of the Mist,11</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Matapo, ... 2. Native rat. <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,220</i>)

>  native rat n, NewZeal : KIORE <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1506</i>)

>  Hinamoki = inamoki, n. 1. Native rat (Rattus exulans). <i>(1971,Dictionary of the Maori Language,51</i>)

>  <b>MIKAERE</b> In those days the haumata (snow grass) grew over one and half metres high, and abounded with ... succulent kiore (native rats). <i>(1988,Te Maiharoa,75</i>)

>  For many years the 'native rat' (looking rather like a big house mouse) was thought to be harmless, particularly in comparison to its larger, more sinister European-introduced cousins - the Norway rat and the ship rat. <i>(Nov 2002,Forest & Bird,306/11</i>)

### Native reserve
 
 <i>n.</i> later Maori reserve. [See Native Reserves Act ?1856]

>  At the lottery for priority of choice, the native reserves proved greatly to exceed the average of fortune. <i>(Aug 21, 1839,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,7</i>)

>  NO SAWYER or other person will be permitted to cut timber or erect houses on any NATIVE reserve. <i>(Oct 17, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)

>  TENDERS ARE INVITED for leasing Section 23 of the Native Reserve on the south bank of the Hokitika River for 21 years from the 1st July, 1902 and also for Native reserve sections 18, 18A and 18B of the said reserve, including the right to mine for gold and other metals thereon, for 10 years from the 1st July, 1902. <i>(Jun 14, 1902,West Coast Times,3</i>)

>  LOT 2:- Leasehold Section 11, Block 2 Greymouth Native Reserve with Hotel known as "Brian Boru" thereon. <i>(Nov 27, 1920,Grey River Argus,1</i>)

>  SALE BY AUCTION .. At 2 p.m., in the Mart, Wharf Street - Lease of Section No. 55, Native Reserves, Arahura. <i>(Jan 9, 1891,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  <b>RUTHERFORD</b> It strikes me that a portion of the funds derived from Native Reserves might be very suitably setapart for the payment of outside Protectors and the promotion of Christian Missions; ... <i>(1949,Selected Documents,15A</i>) [NOTES: 1846 Jan 20 E.G. Wakefield to Gladstone in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 15A]

>  After travelling twenty-one miles ... they came to a native reserve on the Taieri (Ty-ee-ree) river, where there are but two white men squatting, with permission, on the native land. <i>(Dec 17, 1848,Letters from Otago,24,1978 ed.</i>)

>  Restarted at 10 a.m. and steered WNW. past the bushes - saw some of the marks left by Messrs Mantell and Wills for native reserves. <i>(Dec 20, 1848,Torlesse Papers,43,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The purchasers then received [in London] land-orders entitling them to choose one Town-section and one rural section for each land-order, in the rotation governed by thenumber drawn for it; and the native reserves took their chance with the rest. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,175</i>)

>  There are two routes to Oxford, or Harewood Forest, ...; the one by the pah of Kiapoi, or the Native reserve, the other, and the straighter one, being a direct line, ... <i>(Jan 25, 1851,Lyttelton Times,6</i>)

>  <b>ROCHFORT</b> I accompanied ... the government land commissioner ... to pay ... the natives for the purchase of their land ..., and make a survey of the native reserves. [fn] All the land of any use is purchased by the government, except a sufficient quantity to support the aborigines, which is called a native reserve. <i>(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,31</i>)

>  <b>MACKIE</b> Portions of land have been set apart for their use, called `native reserves', in all the settlements. <i>(Jul 14, 1853,Traveller under Concern,122,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>RUTHERFORD</b> As the maintenance of uniformity in the mode of disposing of the waste lands of the Colony would not seem to be of essential importance, <i>(1949,Selected Documents,59</i>) [NOTES: 1854 May 27 Address of Officer administering the Government to the General Assembly in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 59]

>  <b>SEWELL</b> One great wood about 2000 Acres is a Native Reserve. <i>(Jan 1854,Journal,1/425,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>D'AVIGDOR</b> There are now `Native Reserves' in both islands, the increasing revenues from which are administered by a minister for the benefit of the various tribes interested, who are yet tacitlyacknowledged by everybody to be as unable to look after their own property as children or lunatics. <i>(1888,Antipodean Notes,105</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> A party of adventuresome white people in 1860 ... arrived at the Buller ... expressed their desire to return to civilisation, and Mackay, after fixing up some Native Reserves at the Buller, agreed to shepherd them to Massacre bay. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,1</i>)

>  <b>SELWYN</b> ... I well remember ... in my character as trustee of the native reserves, I requested Mr.Thompson to act as local manager of the Nelson Estate ... <i>(1851,New Zealand (Selwyn),Part V (Church in the Colonies XX) 49</i>) [NOTES: 1848 June 23 Selwyn in 1851 NZ]

>  Suppose.. that 10,000 acres of this 100,000 is to be a 'native reserve,' this reserve should be made inalienable. <i>(Aug 23, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> A party of adventuresome white people in 1860 ... arrived at the Buller ... expressed their desire to return to civilisation, and Mackay, after fixing up some Native Reserves at the Buller, agreed to shepherd them to Massacre bay. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,1</i>)

### native sandalwood
 
 <i>n.</i> maire

>  The process woud be closely analagous to that exhibited by the native sandalwood (<i>Fusanus cunninghamii</i>), and the `eyebright' (<i>Euphrasia cuneata</i>) ... <i>(1895,TrNZI,28/497</i>)

>  <i>Fusanus Cunninghamii</i> native sandalwood <i>(1896,TrNZI,28/</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

### Native school
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> This morning I attended the native school men women and children there were about 200 present arranged in circles the women apart around the female teachers some writing some learning their letters others repeating the catechism... <i>(Apr 27, 1839,Journal,2/118</i>) [NOTES: 1839 Apr 27 Taylor Journal [TS:ATB]]

>  <b>SELWYN</b> Beyond the chapel ... a handsome wooden building contains the masters and scholars of thenative school ... <i>(1851,New Zealand (Selwyn),Part V (Church in the Colonies XX) 18</i>) [NOTES: 1848 June 23 Selwyn in 1851 NZ]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> This is the work of our native schools, and has occupied forty spades, great and small - the older [men] digging, the younger ... pulverizing the soil. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,378</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Annals of the Diocese of NZ 223 in 1848 Wakefield Handbook for NZ 378]

>  <b>RUTHERFORD</b> 1 During the session of the General Assembly, which has just closed, the following measures, on subjects specially affecting the aboriginal natives of New Zealand, have passed both Houses:- ... 5. The Native Schools Act. <i>(1949,Selected Documents,104</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Sep 29 Memorandum by Responsible Advisers on Native Affairs in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 104.]

>  <b>RUTHERFORD</b> The runangas will also have the following powers. I. Of inspecting and reporting on native schools aided by Government grants ... <i>(1949,Selected Documents,112b</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Nov 2 Sir George Grey Memorandum in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 112b]

>  <b>HARPER</b> At Arahura there is a Native School, attended also by a few European children. <i>(Jan 14, 1884,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,214,1914 ed.</i>)

>  [all small even caps] NUMBER OF PUBLIC (PRIMARY) SCHOOLS, COLLEGES, GRAMMAR AND HIGH SCHOOLS, PRIVATE SCHOOLS, INDUSTRIAL SCHOOLS,ORPHANAGES, AND NATIVE SCHOOLS, IN THE COLONY OF NEW ZEALAND, ON 31ST DECEMBER, 1890 ... <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,107</i>)

>  Applications from Married Teachers for Appointments to Native Schools at Waima (Hokianga District) ... will be received up to the 3rd prox. <i>(Mar 1, 1902,NZ Journal of Education,4/1</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> If the annual report of the native schools of New Zealand is pervaded with the same commonsense as the excerpts ... there should be nothing to carp at; ... <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,230</i>)

>  Tried by another standard ... the Native-school system is not so satisfactory. In the first place, the school is a `Native school'; the race-distinction is emphasized from the start ... <i>(1908,TrNZI,40/171</i>)

>  <b>GRIEVE</b> We had been appointed to a Native School in North Auckland. <i>(1939,Sketches from Maoriland,15,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> .. a memorial, the inscription on which, translated for me ... by Jerry Te Haara, head boy of the neighbouring native school, ... <i>(1946,Farthest North,16</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> As Inspector of native schools, I was much attracted by the old type of Maori rangatira, both men and women ... <i>(1948,Taina,vii</i>)

>  The native schools system in New Zealand lasted from 1867 to 1969, when the remaining schools were absorbed into the State school system. Until recently, the few works on native schooling (renamed "Maori" in 1947) were based mainly on official sources. <i>(Aug 17, 2001,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  It is 30 years since the state-fundedsystem of Native Schools (later calledMaori Schools) ceased to exist. <i>(Jan 31, 1993,Sunday Times (NZ),15</i>)

>  The Peria native school was examined.. on Friday. <i>(Jan 4, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,18</i>)

>  I advocated.. a native school, and other essential accomodation. <i>(Nov 10, 1938,NZ Observer,6</i>)

>  The atmosphere of a Native school triumphs over the alien culture. <i>(1945,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),III(9) : 32</i>)

>  My wife and I want to ask your advice about Native Schools... [He] was soon busy answering a fusillade of questions about the conditions in the Native Service. <i>(Jun 5, 1947,NZ Woman's Weekly: The First 60 Years,3</i>)

>  I remember one busy week shortly after I had been appointed to a small native school, when I was asked to perform the burial service for no fewer than four people - one adult and three children. <i>(Jan 14, 1944,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>) [NOTES: Reprinted in <i>Listener</i>, 15/1/1994, p. 9]

>  There was a good native school. <i>(Jan 1, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,10</i>)

### Native school teacher
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The heavy expenses incurred by many of the native school teachers in travelling to Auckland from remote backblock stations affords an illuminating commentary on the sacrifices that are made by so many teachers. <i>(Jan 5, 1949,Auckland Weekly News,22</i>)

### Native Secretary
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  For the future, the duties of the NativeSecretary will be performed by.. theCivil Secretary. <i>(Aug 10, 1848,NZ Gazette,1(19) : 1</i>) [NOTES: This is the Gazette for the Province of New Munster]

>  <b>RUTHERFORD</b> In justice, however, to that zealous and experienced gentleman, the native secretary, I attach some remarks of his. <i>(1949,Selected Documents,103b</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Oct 14 Governor Gore Browne to Sir E. BulwerLytton in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents]

>  .. Mr. MacLean (the Native Secretary), is reported to have arrived at Hawke's Bay, ... <i>(Aug 26, 1957,Lyttelton Times,3</i>)

>  <b>LADY MARTIN</b> He was a short, excitable, wild-looking man, and threw out dark hints to the Native Secretary that, if favour was shown to the prisoner, it might become unsafe for travellers to pass through his district. <i>(1884,Our Maoris,61</i>)

### Native Service
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  My wife and I want to ask your advice about Native Schools... [He] was soon busy answering a fusillade of questions about the conditions in the Native Service. <i>(Jun 5, 1946,NZ Woman's Weekly: The First 60 Years,3</i>)

### Native settlement
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical)

>  The medical students are being distributed throughout the native settlements. <i>(Sep 2, 1913,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  On descending the range, we entered a valley of considerable extent, to the left of which was a native settlement. The inhabitants having, a short time previously, left for Paupo, we found no one there but an old woman, who luckily had a good supply of corn and potatoes, and in whose hut I managed to sleep very comfortably. <i>(Aug 26, 1842,New Zealand Colonist & Port Nicholson Advertiser,4</i>)

>  His Excellency visited every native settlement in the district; all the natives said, that if Aramakaraka was willing, peace would be made. <i>(Apr 17, 1855,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  A pah is strictly a fortified village, but it has ceased to be applied to a fortified one only, and a collection of huts forming a native settlement is generally termed a pah now a days, and the pah I refer to as "Our pah" has no signs of fortifications about it. <i>(Sep 11, 1862,Wellington Independent,5</i>)

>  Further, the Maoris at the native settlement, Okatire, seven miles up the river by water, say they can no longer get fresh water, as they could until recently, out of the river, the scour caused by the harbor works enabling the tide to run up much further. <i>(Mar 27, 1880,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  It was at the native settlement of Ruatoki, situated within thirty miles of that historic Church at Opotiki, that one of the most inspiring conferences of Maori Church leaders was held, from Wednesday evening, May 21st, to Sunday evening, 25th. <i>(Jul 1, 1930,Waiapu Church Gazette,13</i>)

>  On the return of a detachment of the Militia from the front, while passing through the native settlement of Tamahin, they were strongly urged by one of the chiefs to dispose of part of their ammunition, and even their arms. <i>(Feb 19, 1870,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  Some years ago a typhoid epidemic broke out at Omarumutu, a small native settlement east of Opotiki, Bay of Plenty, when many natives were swept off the surface of the earth. <i>(Jul 9, 1897,Colonist (Nelson),4</i>)

>  Dr. Pomare, the Government Native Health Officer, carefully examined the various Native settlements during his visit and impressed on the people inhabiting them the necessity of more careful attention to sanitary matters. <i>(May 13, 1903,Ohinemuri Gazette,2</i>)

### native snipe
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 83. GALLINAGO PUSILLA. Buller. Native snipe. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,31</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> The birds I noticed were the bell-bird, the blight-bird, the yellow-headed tit, the ground lark, the little parakeet, the banded dotterel, and the native snipe. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,275,1933 ed.</i>)

### native snowdrop
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A few flowers and leaves of a plant known as the "native snowdrop" have been sent.. with the following note:- "The plant's botanical name is Arthropodium candidum." <i>(Mar 2, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

### Native spear
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ROCHFORT</b> I had a native spear with me for a walking stick, which I carried as a soldier does a fixed bayonet. <i>(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,39</i>)

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> ... a number of Maoris, some armed with muskets, and others with hatchets and native spears,followed us. <i>(1940,Matthews of Kaitaia,43</i>) [NOTES: 1833 Mar letter in 1940 Matthews Matthews of Kaitaia]

### native spinach
 
 <i>n.</i> Tetragonia tetragonioides (See [New Zealand spinach](../N#new-zealand-spinach))

>  Our native spinach, <i>Tetragonia tetragonoides</i>, has fleshy, pointed leaves that grow up gangly stems rather than in a tight clumps [sic] like traditional spinach. <i>(May 23, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),15</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> There are the native spinach or Renga-renga (<i>Tetragonia expansa </i>), the Pana-pana, or cress (<i>Cardamine hirsuta </i>), and the Reti-reti, or sorrel (<i>Oxalis [211] magellanica </i>), which do for salad and green vegetables. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/210</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i> Tetragonia trigyna </i> NATIVE SPINACH ... Kookihi ... Rather succulent sprawling herb ... Stems often red-stained. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,146</i>)

>  A good companion would be the scrambling native spinach, kokihi or tetragonia tetragonioides ... <i>(Dec 24, 2004,Dominion Post,C9</i>)

### Native station
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori pa

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> We arrived early in the afternoon at the native station near Lake Waiwera. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,245</i>)

### Native teacher
 
 <i>n.</i> a mission teacher of Christian doctrine who is a Maori.

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> They seemed pleased with the proposal, and I told them that if a European should not come we would supply them with native teachers, which we shall, at all events, have in our power. <i>(Dec 4, 1832,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1832 Dec 4 W.Williams's Journal in 1940 Matthews Matthews of Kaitaia 33]

>  <b>SELWYN</b> The native teacher [at Kaiaua native village] brought me two little boys ... with an earnest request that I would take them with me to school [in Auckland]. <i>Ibid.</i> 101 A small class of candidates were confirmed ... but I could not satisfy myself with those who were presented by the native teachers for baptism ... <i>(1851,New Zealand (Selwyn),Part V (Church in the Colonies XX) 63</i>) [NOTES: 1848 Aug 30 Selwyn in 1851 NZ]

>  <b>WILEY & MAUNSELL</b> We are in need of a dormitory or rather two or three dormitories for children besides homes for native teachers. <i>(1938,Robert Maunsell,183</i>) [NOTES: 1849 Mar 19 in Wily and Maunsell Robert Maunsell (1938)]

>  <b>GRACE</b> Native Teachers. - I am unable to report very favourably of Native Teachers. <i>(1928,Grace Pioneer Missionary,17</i>) [NOTES: 1851 Dec 31 T.S.Grace Report of Turanga District in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928)]

>  <b>BUDDLE</b> ... a Native Teacher then read a portion of Scripture, sang a Hymn, and engaged in prayer, after which Te Heuheu chanted a song of welcome ... <i>Ibid.</i> 19 Another subject that illustrates the native desire for independence is the great change that has taken place in their views in reference to Native Teachers of religion. <i>(1860,Maori King Movement,14</i>)

>  <b>LADY MARTIN</b> One of the native teachers said: `Though the heavens are black around us, this is the bright spot of blue sky, which gives hope that the storm will soon pass away.' <i>(1884,Our Maoris,48</i>)

### Native team
 
 <i>n.</i> a 19C touring Maori rugby team (see 1992 cit.)

>  The mortality amongst the Native team that journeyed to the Old Country has been remarkable. <i>(Oct 8, 1904,NZ Observer,5</i>)

>  Joe Warbrick.. was appointed captain of the New Zealand Native team that toured the United Kingdom in 1888/89, even though the team included four Maori players. <i>(Jan 1992,Metro,78</i>)

>  <b>PALENSKI</b> The two [Eyton and Warbrick] pooledresources and ideas and the Native tourwas born - retaining the Native namealthough four pakehas were included. <i>(1992,Our National Game,21</i>)

### native thorn
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Discaria toumatou</i>, tumatakuru (matagouri)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Thorn, native. <i>Discaria Toumatou. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> DISCARIA TOUMATOU (<i>Raoul.</i>) ... The `TOUMATOU'.- ... The plant is very generally known throughout the Colony as `The Wild Irishman'. In the Middle Island, more particularly towards Otago, it is called `Native Thorn'. <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,93</i>)

### native thrush
 
 <i>n.</i> [piopio]

>  The peculiar huia, now nearly if not quite extinct, abounded; the beautiful native crow, or kokako, favoured certain spots; the now long-lost native thrush, or piopio, flourished ... <i>(Oct 17, 1925,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  .. the native quail inhabited the more open fern country near Island Bay and in the Kaiwarra Gorge, and probably in other gorges running down to the harbour, there were such rare birds as the stitch-bird, native thrush, and others. <i>(Aug 3, 1940,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Korohea (<i>Turnagra Hectori</i>) is the native thrush, and a poor imitation it is of the English throstle. It is scarce. Sometimes its song may be listened to with pleasure. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/224</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Piopio ... Turnagra tanagra, native thrush. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,327</i>)

>  He remembers the time when the native thrush, the saddle-back, and the orange-wattled crow were abundant in the Inangahua Valley, but he has seen none of these species for twenty years. <i>(Feb 19, 1910,Evening Post,13</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

### Native title
 
 <i>n.</i> = Maori title [NATIVE [MAORI] (CUSTOMARY) TITLE. A fascinating and still proceeding investigation. The phrase is not defined in the acts - Native is but not Native Title. There are at least two types of Native (Maori) title - customary, which I presume means communal (of the iwi), and freehold. There will undoubtedly be more colloquial references in second readings to bills. The earliest reference is quoted from "1847 NZPCC 387 n at 390" which I will need to consult with a lawyer about - the book in question doesn't have an explanation of NZPCC. (HWO note from 1990s)]

>  In such a large and important matter asdealing with the Native title, such apower should be given to the Judges. <i>(1873,NZ Parliamentary Debates,XIV/609</i>)

>  To declare, as some people declare, thatthe situation of the Native-landdifficulty is to individualise everyNative-land title, and allow the Nativeto sell his land and become landless, iscounsel not only of folly but also ofweakness. <i>(1909,NZ Parliamentary Debates,CXLVIII/1273</i>)

>  "Native land" was land in the Colonyowned by Natives under their customs andusages, the title whereto has not beenascertained by the Court]. <i>(1894,NZ Statutes,58 Vict. 43/312 Sect. 2</i>)

>  <b>HINDE, McMORLAND & SIM</b> In solemnly guaranteeing the Nativetitle, and in securing what is called theQueen's pre-emptive right, the Treaty ofWaitangi, confirmed by the Charter of theColony, does not assert either indoctrine, or in practice, any thing newand unsettled. <i>(1986,Introduction to Land Law,16, Sect. 1.019</i>)

>  A Proclamation by the Governor that anyland vested in His Majesty the King isfree from the Native customary titleshall in all courts and in allproceedings be accepted as conclusiveproof of the fact so proclaimed. <i>(1909,NZ Statutes,9 Edw. VIII 15/181 Sect. 85</i>)

>  The bed of the lake known as Lake Taupo,and the bed of the Waikato River.. arehereby declared to be the property of theCrown, freed and discharged from Nativecustomary title (if any) or any otherNative freehold title thereto. <i>(1926,NZ Statutes,17 Geo. V 64/697, Sect. 14(1)</i>) [NOTES: Native Land Amendment & NativeLand Claims Adjustment Act 1926]

>  <b>SALMOND</b> The customary Native title to the land ofNew Zealand has now for the most partbeen extinguished. <i>(1932,Public Acts of NZ,VI/87</i>) [NOTES: 1909 in 1932]

>  The Maori customary title shall be deemedto have been lawfully extinguished inrespect of all land which.. wascontinuously in the possession of theCrown. <i>(1953,NZ Statutes,94/1143, Sect. 57(2)</i>) [NOTES: Maori Affairs Act, 1953]

>  The transformation of Maori customarytitle into freehold titles was requiredto be carried out by the Maori Land Courtuno ictu. <i>(May 4, 1976,NZ Law Journal,9/205</i>)

>  <b>HINDE, McMORLAND & SIM</b> [Hdg] Maori customary title and theMaori Land Court. Upon the assumption ofsovereignty over New Zealand the Crownacquired not only title to all land inNew Zealand, but also the exclusive rightof extinguishing Maori title, either bythe free conquest of the Maori occupiersor by legislation. <i>(1986,Introduction to Land Law,18, Sect. 1.020</i>)

>  <b>von HAAST</b> The practice of extinguishing Nativetitles by fair purchases is certainlymore than two centuries old... It is nowpart of the law of the land, and althoughthe Courts of the United States, in suitsbetween their own subjects, will notallow a grant to be impeached underpretext that the Native title has notbeen extinguished, yet they wouldcertainly not hesitate to do so in a suitof one of the Native Indians... Insolemnly guaranteeing the Native title,and in securing what is called theQueen's pre-emptive right, the Treaty ofWaitangi, confirmed by the Charter of theColony, does not assert either indoctrine, or in practice, any thing newand unsettled... The right which residesin the Crown is.. the exclusive right ofextinguishing the Native title. <i>(1938,NZ Privy Council Cases,390</i>) [NOTES: 1847 in 1938. It is likely that there are earlierreferences in US law to this concept butfrom the remarks made in the abovejudgment, it seems that these were called"Indian titles", not "Native titles".]

>  Any notification.. stating that theNative title over any land being a Nativereserve therein described wasextinguished previously to a date thereinspecified, shall for all purposes beconclusive proof that the Native titleover the land described in such noticewas extinguished at some time previouslyto the date therein specified. <i>(1873,NZ Statutes,37 Vict. 60/279, Sect. 40</i>) [NOTES: The Native Reserves Act, 1873]

>  <b>von HAAST</b> All Native titles by Native Custom wereextinguished. <i>(1938,NZ Privy Council Cases,402</i>) [NOTES: 1901 in 1938]

>  The proceedings commenced by bringing ina claim for investigation of the Nativetitle. <i>(1938,NZ Privy Council Cases,284</i>) [NOTES: 1904-05 in 1938]

>  It embraces several important collateral[sic] points, such as the "extinction ofnative title", the distinction between"Crown lands and the demesne lands of theCrown," "reparation or remedy andpenalty". <i>(Sep 13, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  Claimants have argued the land was for food gathering and the cultivation and that native title to the land was never extinguished. <i>(May 16, 2003,Dominion Post,A6</i>)

>  [The judgement] embraces several important collateral points, such as.. the "extinction of native title," the distinction between "Crown lands and the demesne lands of the Crown". <i>(Sep 13, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

### native tobacco
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Verbascum thapsus</i>;flannel leaf; velvet; golden rod; an occasional name for mullein

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> FLANNEL LEAF (<i>Verbascum thapsus</i>) is commonly called mullein in England, and is frequently so called here as well. Its commonest local name is probably tobacco plant, or native tobacco, a name for which there is no justification in either appearance or use. Velvet mullein woolly mullein, golden rod, and Aaron's rod are other appellations, but flannel leaf, one of the common sheep-station names is both descriptive and distinctive. The plant is found in dry soil and river beds ... in both islands. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,155,1967 ed.</i>)

>  5. VERBASCUM THAPSUS, a weed of poor land has many popular names. In New Zealand it is called native tobacco, velvet, flannel leaf and golden rod. <i>(Nov 22, 1974,Dominion</i>)

>  From Waikato despatches is gathered the notice that a kind of native tobacco is found growing in some of the abandoned Maori Stations. <i>(May 3, 1864,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  The Kawhia natives have never experienced any difficulty in the cultivation of the tobacco plant, the only difficulty being how to render the "weed" most enjoyable. <i>(Apr 30, 1873,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  About three or four weeks since, our attention was directed to a sample of Native tobacco, grown at Wakatane, in the Bay of Plenty. At that time it was raw and uncured, twisted up on rools not unlike, in colour and appearance, to ropes of native flax. <i>(Oct 5, 1852,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

### Native township
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It [the new bridge] will cross the Waipa at Whatiwhatihoe, and literally joins together the European and Maori townships, bringing Finch's hotel in a line with the native township, a facility which, judging by last night's proceedings, will be largely taken advantage of. <i>(May 1, 1882,Hawke's Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  Can the Government prevent Europeans from at least feuing land in these native townships? <i>(Oct 28, 1843,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  The land at the proposed native township is high, overlooking the river, being the termination of the fern range from riverhead to the Kaipara. <i>(Jul 22, 1864,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  House, Garden, and 16 Allotments of Land, being sub-division of Sections nos. 200 and 201, Native Township, fronting Herbert street and Chapel street west. <i>(Jan 17, 1873,Grey River Argus,3</i>)

>  The only residents near are one stationholder, the inhabitants of a small Native pah and a Native township at Omaio Bay. <i>(Nov 11, 1921,Ashburton Guardian,5</i>)

>  1895 NZ Statutes 59 Vict. 12/29An Act to provide the Settlement andOpening-up of the Interior of the NorthIsland... The Short Title of this Act is"The Native Townships Act, 1895." <i>(1895,NZ Statutes,59 Vict. 12/29</i>)

>  In every Native township there shall bereserved and laid off for the use of theNative owners such allotments,hereinafter called "Native allotments,"not exceeding in the whole an area oftwenty per centum of the total area ofthe township... It shall be the duty ofthe Surveyor-General to include in suchreservations every Native burying-ground,and every building actually occupied by aNative at the date of the gazetting ofthe Proclamation. <i>(1895,NZ Statutes,59 Vict. 12/30</i>)

>  An Act to amend "The Native TownshipsAct, 1895." <i>(1903,NZ Statutes,3 Edw. 33/69</i>)

>  Subject to the provisions of this Act,all Native land situated in a Nativetownship under the Native Township Act1895.. shall.. become vested for a legalestate in fee-simple, without grant orother assurance, in the Maori Land Boardof the district in which that township issituated. <i>(1910,NZ Statutes,1 Geo. V 18/48</i>)

>  Any land acquired by the Crown in anyNative Township.. may be disposed of byway of sale, lease, or otherwise inaccordance with the regulations madeunder section twenty of that Act. <i>(1919,NZ Statutes,10 Geo. V 22/67</i>)

>  An Act to consolidate and amend the lawrelating to the Administration of thelands comprised in Maori reserves, WestCoast settlement reserves, and "Maori"townships... This Act may be cited as theMaori Reserves Act 1955. <i>(1955,NZ Statutes,I 38/264</i>)

>  Mr. Lang (Waikato) asked the NativeMinister, whether the Native townships inthe King-country would be opened at anearly date? <i>(1903,NZ Parliamentary Debates,127/854</i>)

>  It is only by purchasing land from theNatives, and giving the lessees or theirsuccessors a freehold title, that youwill get these Native townships liftedout of the ruck they are in at thepresent moment. <i>(1910,NZ Parliamentary Debates,151/275</i>)

>  For example, there are the Maoritownships at Tokaanu, Te Kuiti, andOtorohanga. <i>(1955,NZ Parliamentary Debates,307/2947</i>)

### Native track
 
 <i>n.</i> = Maori track

>  <b>COWAN</b> Native tracks, as a rule, follow the best lines of the country, but are generally capable of being improved in detail. <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,106</i>) [NOTES: 1869 Oct 29 Memorandum from Native Minister in 1940 Cowan Sir Donald Maclean 106]

>  <b>RIESCHEK</b> I sent Caesar after him, but because the native track was exceedingly narrow, the dog could not get ahead of the horse. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,155,1933 ed.</i>)

### native tree daisy
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The native tree daisy - Oharia [<i>sic</i>] gardnerii - is no oil painting, but Department of Conservation staff are thrilled after 15 new species were found, increasing the gene pool for propagation. Until now there weree only 80 known trees, mostly in a DOC-managed reserve near Taihape. <i>(May 8, 2002,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  Members of Doc's Biodiversity Wairarapa team recently discovered 15 critically endangered native tree daisies (Olearia gardnerii), a discovery likened to discovering 15 new kakapo... The tree daisy is a small tree, growing only five or six metres. It has a short lifespan of around fifty years. <i>(May 22, 2002,Capital Times,16</i>)

### native trout
 
 <i>n.</i> = kokopu [Freshwater <i> Galaxias</i> spp., esp. <i> G. argenteus</i> or <i> G. fasciatus<i/>, KOKOPU.]

>  <b>McDOWALL</b> The fourth species in the catch is rarely seen, and is the giant kokopu (<i>Galaxias argenteus</i>), again commonly known as Maori trout or native trout. <i>(1984,NZ Whitebait Book, The,14</i>)

>  <b>SPEIGHT</b> The species of <i>Galaxias</i> may broadly be divided into the small, sea-running whitebait minnows (<i>Galaxias attenuatus</i>), the larger native `trout' (<i>G. fasciatus</i> and allied species), which probably breed inland, and an aberrant species of medium size (<i>G. borrowsii</i>) which approaches the Westland mudfish (<i>Neochanna apoda</i>) in form....The larger mottled brown native trout occur in most of the streams and lakes of Canterbury. Their breeding habits are little known from actual observation, but from the lack of evidence of a seaward migration it is inferred that they breed in the streams and lakes. <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,196</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 196]

>  <i>Galaxias fasciatus</i>: [European Name] Native trout or mountain trout. [Maori Name] Kokopu. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,73</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Eels, crayfish and cockabullies (native trout) were plentiful in some streams, but were very seldom eaten [in Otago in the 1860s]. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,122</i>)

>  Among fishes, the `native trout' (<i>Galaxias fasciatus</i>). <i>(1939,TrRSNZ,69/125</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Fifty years ago the Waimate Creek was full of native trout, or Kokopuru; ... <i>(1940,Te Waimate,241,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Kokopu - throughout these lists the translator [sc Beattie] has translated this as `native trout'. A good-sized one would weigh 1lb. The translator has eaten one and found the flesh sweet, but it was full of bones. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,63</i>)

>  <b>LYON</b> .. to wade up the clear gully stream in pursuit of the small koura, or crayfish, and any native trout or kokopu we could spear, or guddle. <i>(1952,Faring South,79</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> MINNOW or WHITEBAIT (INANGA) <i>Austrocobitis attenuatus</i> (Jenyns) ... For a long time it has been known to the Maori and to Europeans that our New Zealand Whitebait is the young of a native freshwater fish commonly known as Minnow, or Inanga or Hiwi to the Maori, and erroneously called a Trout. ... The early settlers called it Maori Trout, Native Trout, Rock Trout and Minnow. But it is not a Trout. Nor is it a Minnow, through it resembles the English freshwater Minnow. While the name Trout is not correct, it is and will always be known as such by farmers and naturalists. In the Bay of Plenty, Whitebait is known to the Maori as Porohoe. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,118</i>)

>  Sometimes the banded and giant galaxias are called `native trout', but they are not related to the trout and the former names are more appropriate. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/235</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> There was an accommodating pool here well stocked with <i>kokopu</i>. ... These little native trout soon became tame ... <i>(1970,Life's Vagaries,44</i>)

>  <b>OGILVIE</b> Other pioneers in the Opihi district [of Canterbury] refer to the abundance of native pigeons, wood hens, native trout, fresh water crayfish, eels and Maori rats - which were small, grey, fluffy, vegetarian and evidently succulent. <i>(1971,Moonshine County,131</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> MINNOW or WHITEBAIT (INANGA) <i>Austrocobitis attenuatus</i> (Jenyns) ... For a long time it has been known to the Maori and to Europeans that our New Zealand Whitebait is the young of a native freshwater fish commonly known as Minnow, or Inanga or Hiwi to the Maori, and erroneously called a Trout. ...The early settlers called it Maori Trout, Native Trout, Rock Trout and Minnow. But it is not a Trout. Nor is it a Minnow, through it resembles the English freshwater Minnow. While the name Trout is not correct, it is and will always be known as such by farmers and naturalists. In the Bay of Plenty, Whitebait is known to the Maori as Porohoe. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,118</i>)

>  The "bully" or "bull-head," and the kokopu (sometimes called the "native trout," a very lazy fish) are found in rivers and streams. <i>(1909,NZ School Journal,3(10) : 330</i>)

>  There is an old coalpit at Lovells Flat operations in connection with which have been suspended for many years. The pit is chock full of water - in fact it overflows and a lagoon has been formed above and about it. Where the water comes from nobody knows. No stream enters it, and it has no connection with any running water. It must be an accumulation of rain water. Yet it swarms with cockabulli (native trout), and the fresh water crayfish. <i>(Sep 4, 1891,Bruce Herald,2</i>)

>  The bully, or bull-head, and the kokopu, or native trout, live entirely in fresh water. <i>(Nov 13, 1901,Star, The,4</i>)

>  The <i>Lyttelton Times</i> says it would appear that the native trout, better known by many as the grayling, is being exterminated. <i>(Aug 25, 1871,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  Three fine specimens of these now rare fish, the native trout, the largest 1a lb, have been added to the many imported varieties which may be seen at the Opawa fish-breeding establishment, Canterbury. <i>(Nov 15, 1889,Hawke's Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  Then there was the stream with its native trout and deep bathing pools. <i>(Jan 5, 1917,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,2</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a Maori pa

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> For this purpose we took two native guides and set off on our journey. ... We arrived at a native village just at dark, very wet, where we took up our lodgings for the night, ... <i>(1820,Letters & Journals of Samuel Marsden,251,1932 ed.</i>)

### Native war
 
 <i>n.</i> = New Zealand war(s).

>  With his death ended the native war in Taranaki. <i>(Aug 7, 1890,Otago Witness,36</i>)

[He] appears to have ben one of the speakers who attended the meeting of chiefs at Wellington in April last - a meeting convened ... for the purpose of hearing the views of the native chiefs in reference to the Taranaki War ... <i>(1860,Maori King Movement,6</i>)

>  THE NATIVE WAR. GREAT VICTORY AT POVERTY BAY <i>(Jan 9, 1869,Grey River Argus,3</i>)

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> ... where a few huts apologised for the absence of a city, a large and healthy town has gradually risen: and though checked somewhat by the cost of the native war ... which is now happily at anend,- the Southern Island ... has still ... many years of prosperous days before her. <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,225</i>)

### native-born
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  In fact, there are many native-born New-Zealanders who could not distinguish between a rimu and a kauri ... <i>(1894,TrNZI,26/621</i>)

With that generation also had largely passed the pakeha who was wedded to the idea of England being `Home'. ### natural
 
 <i>adj.</i> Snowboarding term for "left-footed"

### natural boundary
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a cliff, gully, river, gorge or thick bush separating two properties or areas [ possibly retaining stock without fencing. ]

>  <b>BARKER</b> A view of downs rolling into a narrow gully, the flax swamp of which formed a natural boundary. <i>(1871,Christmas Cake in Four Quarters,251</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> We are practically dependent on natural boundaries on all our summer country ... <i>(1972,Sheep Thief,26</i>)

>  Natural Boundary.- Any b. between two stations or two parts of a station which was not made by man. It may be river, bush, or rock, and is often all three. (PAUL, p. 91) <i>(Nov 11, 1933,Press, The,15</i>)

>  <b>CAIRD</b> Wherever it is possible natural boundaries, such as rivers, swamps, and forests, are made use of ... <i>(1874,Notes on Sheepfarming in NZ,18</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> The wilder and rougher it is the more you can depend on 'natural boundaries' - deep gorges, rivers, patches of bush or the tops of high ranges. <i>(1951,NZ High Country,12</i>)

>  <b>MCRAE</b> .. the station relied mainly on natural boundaries: the Hurunui River to the south and the bush-clad mountains to the west. The Genrae and Mandamus rivers provided rather elastic block boundaries, depending on the flow of water. <i>(1963,By the Braes of Balquether,31</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> bull break: a fence across a gully joining natural boundaries such as cliffs. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,164</i>)

### naturalised
 
 <i>adj.</i> (of plant and animal species)

>  Naturalized species are those exotic species that maintain themselves in the wild independent of direct human intervention. <i>(Mar 2003,Open Space,No. 60/20</i>)

### nau
 
 <i>n.</i> Cook's scurvy grass

### ne/nei
 
 <i>exclam.</i> interrogative, giving emphasis to a question, request, or proposal (cf. eh) [ORIGIN: Maori, usu. in Maori-English contexts (ne, nei used in Maori like n'est-ce pas). [Ma. n&emacr. /ne:/]

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> In vain my protestations. `Taihoa, taihoa, all in good time - what's the hurry, O Pakeha!' time was made for slaves - hurry no man's cattle, as we in the classics say. `What's the odds, a day or two sooner or later? There's Motu-Korea; it won't run away - taihoa nei?' with a chuck back of the head as they ended with the interrogative. <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk4/4/269</i>)

>  <b>VOGEL</b> `I will be back ...' [Maori girl answers] `And be with me all the summer, ne?' <i>(1898,Maori Maid,32</i>)

>  <b>VOGEL</b> `You big chief, ne?' the [Maori] woman said in a tone that suggested an assertion rather than a question - though the `ne' is a verbal note of interrogation. <i>(1909,Tragedy of Flirtation,45</i>)

>  <b>ASHTON-WARNER</b> nei? [complete the cit.] <i>(1963,Spinster,70</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> KANI. Dug it,Pera. ... Five feet, almost roast an ox in it [sc. hangi], whole, Time for a beer, ne? [note p. 97] ne: to give emphasis to a question. <i>(1969,Awatea,17,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> HINGAWARU ... When Rangi chooses his wahine, someone else pays, ne? RANGI: When I choose, Dad, I'll choose a girl, not a wahine! Hate that word! Makes me see fat slobbery old tarts waddling along the beach. <i>(1974,Hand on the Rail, The,20</i>) [NOTES: 1981 TS]

>  <b>HULME</b> Then, he had only chuckled again and said, `Well, we got him on the way, nei?' <i>(1983,Bone People, The,5</i>)

>  <b>1986</b> `You hate me now, ne?' the matriarch called. `Yes!' he shouted. `I hurt you, ne?' she asked. <i>(1986,Matriarch, The,132</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> She's proud of that whakapapa. As she's one of the blue-eyed white-blond kind of Maori, she probably feels she needs it, nei? <i>(1986,Te Kaihau,92</i>)

>  <b>Ihimaera</b> 'I suppose you were still trying to protect your papa, ne?' <i>(2007,Ask the Posts,256</i>)

### neenish tart
 
 <i>n.</i> [A small round short-pastry tart with a custard filling but especially characterised by the icing, half of which is black and half white; in Dunedin with a peppermint flavour (check). Other variations in the filling include lemon juice, rum essence or vanilla. Known to me from Dunedin in 1952. An Australian version (see below) is on a biscuit base instead of being in a tart and includes a layer of jam but has the essential bicoloured icing. Possible origins of the name : Nimes (in France); Neen in Shropshire (Neenton, Neen Savage, Neen Sollars); have been told of a Dutch woman making them. There is an outside chance it might be related to the neem tree ("a meliaceous tree of India and Ceylon, sometimes 50 feet high... The natives chew its small twigs and use them as tooth brushes") but I think not. David Burton does not mention them in his books on the raj. Kathryn Walls knows the lemon-flavoured variety from Invercargill (thought they were bakery rather than home-made), Vincent had heard of them, Bill Manhire hadn't. Nelson Wattie does not know them from NZ but when the bicoloured icing was described said he knew them from Germany as "Americans", possibly because of the black and white racial suggestion. Tony Simpson makes, them, his mother made them, his grandmother made them, but he doesn't know anything about the origin. The name appears as a headword in the Macquarie Dictionary but none other that I can find. None of the books on the cookery shelves at National Library has them, not do any of the present stock of second-hand ones in Quilter's but then most modern cookery books are written (or compiled) by restaurant chefs who do not bake and not by bakery cooks.]

>  Neenish Tarts... 4 tablespoons Butter 4 tablespoons Sweetened Condensed Milk 4 tablespoons Icing Sugar 2 dessertspoons Lemon Juice. Soften butter, add sifted icing sugar, condensed milk and lemon juice. When set, ice half the top with white icing and half with chocolate icing. <i>(1982,Budget Macquarie Dictionary,447</i>)

>  Neenish Tarts... 4 tablespoons Butter 4 tablespoons Sweetened Condensed Milk 4 tablespoons Icing Sugar 2 dessertspoons Lemon Juice. Soften butter, add sifted icing sugar, condensed milk and lemon juice. When set, ice half the top with white icing and half with chocolate icing. <i>(1966,Edmonds Cookery Book,30</i>)

>  Neenish Biscuits... Spread half the tops of each biscuit with icing, allow to set. Add sifted cocoa to remaining icing... spread over remaining half of biscuit. <i>(1800,Big Book of Beautiful Biscuits,The,28</i>)

>  Nimish tarts... Ice half chocolate and half white on each. <i>(1800,GHB Cookery Book,22</i>)

>  NENISCHE TARTS. 1 lb. ground almonds, a? lb. icing sugar, 3 tablespoons flour, 4 egg whites, custard as below, icing in two colours. Sift ground almonds; icing sugar and flour. Beat egg white stiffly and add to dry ingredients. Make all to stiff paste. Line tins with this mixture, thinly. Prick with fork, bake in moderate oven till just crisp. Fill with custard and ice each cake with soft white icing and soft brown icing. CUSTARD.- 2 oz. butter, 2 oz. flour, a pint milk, 2oz. sugar, 2 egg yolks, essence to taste. Melt butter, add flour, stir in till smooth and cook one minute. Add milk, stir till boiling and cook 2 or 3 minutes. Add sugar and yolks, and cook without boiling. Cool and add essence. <i>(1942,Aunt Daisy's Cookery Book,5th ed.:</i>)

>  <i>(1968,Edmonds Cookery Book,9th printing</i>) [NOTES: as Neenish Tarts]

>  Neenish Tarts.[This recipe uses raspberry jam in the filling and raspberry coloured icing in place of chocolate. The only recognisable constituent is having a circular iced top in two colours - in this case, white and pink. <i>(1994,Family Circle Tempting Treats</i>) [NOTES: So far no further NZ recipe books have turned it up and it looks as though the 60s might be about the earliest printed occurrence, but I haven't given up hope yet. I have now been through the Turnbull's five boxes without luck.]

>  Stories paint pictures around the recipes, and there are many which will bring back memories for New Zealanders. Remember Neenish tarts, chicken Kiev, roly-poly pudding? <i>(Dec 20, 1997,Dominion,21</i>) [NOTES: A friend suggests this might be a Yiddish word.]

>  I was on my way to a big house, once a children's home. now filled with small ghosts. When I got there I said, "I saw a lake like a Neenish tart." <i>(Jan 8, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)

>  The study of basalt columns on the Chatham Islands.. joins a host of similar images playing with repetition and variation, whether of Neenish tarts or the snows on Ruapehu. <i>(Jun 28, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),57</i>)

### negalitarianism
 
 <i>adj.</i> negative egalitarianism

>  Victoria University political scientist Jon Johansson says "negalitarianism", or negative egalitarianism, is pervasive in our culture. <i>(May 1, 2005,Sunday Star Times,C1</i>)

>  One might conclude, therefore, that Winston [Peters] appeals to that strand of New Zealand political culture that maintains a basic negalitarian outlook towards symbols of wealth and power. <i>(2003,New Zealand Votes: The General Elaction of 2002,67</i>)

### negro
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori, an occas. early derogatory term for a Maori see nigger [a euphemism for J.C.R.'s nigger]

>  Domett has promised to act decisively one way or another about Taranaki ... If we cannot keep the negroes in order it would be most cheerful to clear out and try to get a bit reunited. <i>(Dec 5, 1862,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/807,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: J.C. Richmond, Nelson]

### negrohead
 
 <i>adj.</i> . [negro-head] (See [also niggerhead n1](../A#also-niggerhead-n1))

>  <b>YATE</b> ... for the mere trifle of a fig of negro-head tobacco <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),127</i>) [NOTES: Griffiths Collection]

>  <b>ANGAS</b> We left Tuhua, and on taking leave of our hospitable chief, I gave him five figs of negrohead tobacco (worth about three pence) ... <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/103</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>



 
 <i>n.</i> A tobacco type; NZ use common in early colonial days and as a common payment to a trade with the Maoris; also smoked by early settlers, sailors, stationhands etc. [negro head, negro-head]

>  <b>HODGSKIN</b> I purchased a pig ... for a few negro-heads of tobacco and two gun flints. <i>(1841,Narrative of Eight Months Sojourn in NZ, A,38</i>)

>  <b>McLEAN</b> Improving the road, so as to make it worth his [the Maori chief's] while to erect a turnpike tote, and exact a negrohead - or some other equivalent for every passenger <i>(1845,Papers,1/12</i>)

>  Tobacco is the currency of the Island: you could no more travel without it than in Europe with `the ready'; nothing however but American negro-head will pass; Brazil they [sc. Maoris] will scarcely accept as a gift - ... <i>(1834,NZ Independent,31,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CHAPMAN</b> The proper way to arrange a blanket for the bush is to sew it up like a sack. As to food you carry what you like; and what the host always furnishes is tea (at every meal), damper, i.e. a sort of dough cake baked in the ashes, very good if well made; and fried pork. He can generally furnishthe traveller with a short black pipe and some `negro-head' or Cavendish. <i>(Nov 24, 1847,Letters to His Father, 1843-1851</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Nov 24 H.S. Chapman Letters to His Father [TS,ATB] in 1958 Miller Early Victorian NZ 140]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> tobacco, negro-head, [per] lb, 6-1/2d. to 10d.; ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,180</i>)

>  <b>McLEAN</b> ... payment of 400 sticks of negrohead. Thomson, JT Reconnaissance Survey foetid negrohead. Reed NZ 165 <i>(1851,Papers,IX/199</i>) [NOTES: Halse]

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> TOBACCO. - The `Lion' brand (negrohead) is getting much liked; ... <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/568</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS & REEVES</b> Only think how much more graceful all the landscape will appear. / No more pigroots, whalebacks, hogbacks - these names that shall not last; / ... Negro-heads shall be re-christened. I for one will take no heed, / While to me there yet remaineth Negrohead, the fragrant weed. <i>(1889,Colonial Couplets,3</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> ... foetid negrohead through a `cutty pipe' <i>(1971,Mr Surveyor Thomson,21</i>) [NOTES: Griffiths Collection]



 
 <i>n.</i> a swamp-tussock bush (cf niggerhead, maorihead).

>  <b>WILLIAMS & REEVES</b> Only think how much more graceful all the landscape will appear. / No more pigroots, whalebacks, hogbacks - these names that shall not last; / ... Negro-heads shall be re-christened. I for one will take no heed, / While to me there yet remaineth Negrohead, the fragrant weed. <i>(1889,Colonial Couplets,3</i>)

>  <b>NULLER</b> I cast my eyes for a moment below, and there, in a `negro-head' swamp ... I witnessed a very pretty picture of wild bird life. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/255</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [plant]

### neighbourhood watch
 
 <i>n.</i> [OED gives this as US from 1972]

### neinei
 
 <i>n.</i> A small North Island tree with long tapering leaves. Also called pineapple tree, spider wood. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The surface is covered with stunted nei- nei, Bidwill's Pine, mountain flax, dwarfed forms of tawhero and tawari, and danthonia, brightened up by patches of snowberry. <i>(Jun 15, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  From there, the group takes a well-graded track through red and silver beech, mountai neinei (<i>Dracophyllum traversii</i>), tree daisy (<i>Olearia lacunosa</i>) and mountain cedar to Mt Arthur Hut. <i>(Oct 17, 2000,Dominion,15</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> There are many other woods of much service to Europeans, differing in quality, among others: the 30 <i>Warangai </i> - 31 <i> Pata </i> -32 - <i> Niho </i> - 33 <i> Tangio </i>, - 34 <i> Maihoi </i> - 35 <i> Nene </i> [e's-acute] - 36 <i> Taraiti </i> - 37 <i> Kohihihu </i> 38 <i> Waihupuku </i> - 39 <i> Kaikamoko </i> - 40 <i> Pukapuka </i> - 41 <i> Karanghu </i> - 42 <i> Utuhutu </i> - 43 <i> Angiani </i> - 44 <i> Akkas </i> of various kinds. 45 <i> Parekireki </i>, &c. Of all these woods there are abundant quantities. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/400</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [We found] what was to us quite a new kind of tree, called <i> nei nei </i>. It is something like a diminutive ti. <i>(1959,Early Travellers,355</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Jan 8 Smith Notes of a Journey in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 355]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Neinei, Col[enso]. <i> Dracophyllum latifolium. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> An undescribed, superb, tree-like <i>Dracophyllum </i>, not unlike the <i>Drac. latifolium </i> of the northern island, began to appear here [<i>D. Traversii </i>]. The natives call it Nene. <i>Ibid.</i> 119 There were some very large groves of <i>nene (Dracophyllum traversii </i>), surrounded by a great number of good sized shrubs of Dracophyllum latifolium ... <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,78</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Neinei (<i>Dracophyllum latifolium</i>) is but a small tree. The wood is hard, and is valued for making mallets ... <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/197</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Dracophyllum longifolium</i>, Br. Neinei.--An ornamental shrub-tree with long grassy leaves. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,128</i>)

>  [Native name] Neinei .. [Settlers' name] Neinei .. [Family] Epacrideae [Native name] Neinei .. [Settlers' name] Grass-tree ..[Family] Epacrideae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,122</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 122]

>  Neinei .. Dracophyllum latifolium .. Wood of a reddish brown colour, prettily figured; ... <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,12</i>)

>  Among the strange shrubs [on the West Coast range] the nei-nei is most noticeable, having sometimes growing to thirty feet in height. <i>(1894,NZ Alpine Journal,6/326</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Neinei, n</i>. Maori name for New Zealand shrub, <i>Dracophyllum longifolium</i>, R. Br., also <i>D. traversii</i>, N.O. <i>Epacridea. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,318</i>)

>  <b>VON HAAST</b> <i>(1865,Geology of Westland,78</i>) [NOTES: 1865. J. Von Haast, `A Journey to the West Coast, 1865' (see `Geology of Westland,' p. 78):]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>D[racophyllum latifolium</i>] ... Neinei. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,419</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Dracophyllum latifolium (The Broad-leaved Grass-tree)</i>. A shrub or tree, from 10 ft.- 25 ft. high ... Maori name, Nei-nei. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,330,1940 p. 351 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> We have climbed above the pines [on the West Coast]; ... and up here on the mountain side the vegetation is the flowering <i>rata</i>, the <i>akeake</i>, the native alpine broom, the white blossomed ribbonwood, ... the flax-like <i>kiekie</i>, and the semi-tropical <i>neinei</i>, half-shrub, half-palm, and whole forests of fern trees. <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 95</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> Portions of spear, made of nene (<i>Dracophyllum sp.</i>) .. 1 <i>(1937,Moa-Hunters of NZ,85</i>)

>  <i>nei nei</i> The Maori name for a New Zealand shrub with very long, narrow leaves. (Pronounce <i>nay| nay|</i>). <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),273,8th ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Reference has already been made to the PINE-APPLE TREE of the western uplands of Nelson and Westland, otherwise known as the NEI-NEI or less commonly as the SPIDER-WOOD. <i>(1961,Flora NZ,192,1929 ed.</i>)

>  <i>neinei, nene</i> n. New Zealand ornamental shrub [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),621</i>)

>  <i>neinei, nene</i> ... n. N.Z. ornamental shrub; broad-leaved grass-tree [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),14</i>)

>  <i>neinei n.</i> NZ shrub with long narrow leaves. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,503</i>) [NOTES: nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng.Dict.]

>  The channelled stems of the Neinei (<i>Dracophyllum latifolium</i>), and <i>the ... saplings of Toatoa, or Tanekaha (Phyllocladus trichomanoides)</i>, made valued walking-sticks. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/34</i>) [NOTES: Colenso Botany North Isd]

>  <i>neinei</i>, a shrub (Dracophyllum latifolium), grass-tree. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/154</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> native broom [is this correct.]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Neinei, <i>Lyall. Carmichaelia australis. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

### Nellie bin
 
 <i>n.</i> a fish bin [[note: this term `Nellie bin' is an error. The correct term is `Nally' bin, which is a brand name. Source: Tony Walker, Director, Fishing Industry Services, Christchurch, agent for Nally bins.]]

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> Vessels were often under catch restrictions: no more than four or five baskets (the term 'baskets' was still freely used despite wicker baskets having been replaced by plastic Nellie bins) of species such as trevally and dogfish. <i>(2004,Hooked,305</i>)

### nelly
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Macronectes giganteus</i>, giant petrel,=stinker, =stinkpot (See [giant petrel](../G#giant-petrel))

>  Saw a number of Nellys [petrels] and had a fine chase after several. Killed 5. <i>(1999,Enderby Settlement Diaries,50</i>) [NOTES: Entry for 25 October, 1850]

>  We headed up a great herd of nellies (giant petrel or breakbones). <i>(Jan 30, 1890,Otago Witness,15</i>)

>  We got an egg which contained a full-grown Nelly chick. <i>(Mar 6, 1890,Otago Witness,11</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Ossifraga </i>... or <i> Macronectes ... giganteus </i> ... If a birder comes near the nest the old birds move away a little and the young ones squirt a horrible smelling oil out of their mouths for a distance of 8 ft. Nellies are filthy birds that range the whole of the Southern Hemisphere and deserve their name of stinkpot. Nelly, Stinkpot. TOROA-RURU. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,86</i>)

>  <i>(1955,Cook's Journals,1-60 [fn]</i>)

>  [27b. <i> Macronectes giganteus </i> (Gmelin, 1789) <i> Southern Giant Petrel (Nelly) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,21,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRADY</b> Many giant petrels, larger scavenger birds, known [to the Cook Strait whalers] as Nellies or stinkers, were also found. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,114</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> GIANT PETREL <i> Macronectes giganteus Other names </i>: Nelly, Stinker, Black Molly. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,35</i>)

>  <i> Northern Giant Petrel Macronectes giganteus </i>(GMELIN, 1789) ... OTHER NAMES: <i> Nelly, stinker, giant fulmar. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,68</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> northern giant petrel

>  <i> Northern Giant Petrel Macronectes halli </i> MATHEWS, 1912 ... OTHER NAMES: <i> Nelly, stinker, Hall's giant petrel giant fulmar. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,69</i>)

>  [27. <i> Macronectes halli </i> Mathews 1912] <i> Northern Giant Petrel Nelly </i>) ...ranging widely throughout the southern hemisphere. <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,20,1980 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> giant petrel [[?is this a different bird or merely a synonymous name: CCRansom notes that O. gigantea `giant bonebreaker' is listed nowhere: Giant petrel = giant fulmar]]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 123. OSSIFRAGRA GIGANTEA. Gml. Nelly. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,44</i>)

>  Amongst birds he has obtained [at the Chathams] are ...a Dotterel, differing from the common Dotterel of this country ...; a sea-bird, called by the whalers the `Blue Billy', the beak of which is singularly shaped, and of a blue colour, hence its trivial name; the Nelly <i> Ossifraga gigantea </i>) ... <i>(1872,TrNZI,4/64</i>) [NOTES: Travers Notes on the Chatham Isds]

>  <b>BULLER</b> OSSIFRAGA GIGANTEA. (GIANT PETREL.) ... The Giant Petrel, or `Nelly', as it is called by sailors, is by no means uncommon in our seas, ... <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,297,1888 2nd edn 2/225 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> Of birds still belonging to the Avifauna of New Zealand, the following species were represented :- <i> Graculus punctatus </i>, spotted shag; <i> Eudyptula undina </i>, small blue penguin; <i> Anas superciliosa </i>, grey duck; <i> Graculus carbo </i>, black shag; <i> Graculus varius </i>, pied shag; <i> Graculus brevirostris </i>, white throated shag; <i> Ossifraga gigantea, nelly </i>; <i> Apteryx australis </i>, large kiwi, <i> Nestor meridionalis </i>, kaka; <i> Stringops habroptilus </i>, kakapo, and some other undetermined species. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,417</i>)

>  <i> Ossifraga gigantea </i>, `Nelly,' `Stinkpot.' <i>(1883,TrNZI,15/401</i>) [NOTES: Scott Macquarie Island]

>  <b>DOUGALL</b> [We] had two nellies on board, which are reckoned valuable birds ... <i>(1888,Far South,18</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> A few nellies followed in our wake, eagerly devouring the waste food we threw overboard. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,269,1933 ed.</i>)

>  As we came out [of the Snares Islands], flocks of sea-birds flew and swam around us, andwe headed up a great herd of nellies (<i> Ossifraga gigantea, giant petrel or breakbones), and chased them awhile. <i>(1891,TrNZI,23/492</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> At the very head of the inlet [at the Snares] many young full fledged Nellies (<i> Ossifraga gigantea </i>) crowded together; ... . <i>Ibid.</i> 239 Here and there [in the Bounty Islands] a dark Nelly, or Stinkpot, or Giant Petrel, (<i> Ossifraga gigantea </i>) breasted in single self importance ... <i>(1936,NZ Naturalist,192</i>)

>  <b>BUICK</b> Awl made of distal end of tibia of nelly (<i> Ossifraga gigantea </i>) <i>(1937,Moa-Hunters of NZ,85</i>)

### nelly-birding
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GRADY</b> Nelly-birding - Quite often the Peranos would pursue a pair of whales. If the cow was harpooned first, the bull whale would hang around. The whalers, therefore, tried to get a bomb into a bull in a near vital spot. But sometimes the chaser got out of position, and one of the other chasers would fire their harpoon into it. This was called `Nelly-birding'. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,227</i>)

### Nelson green gecko
 
 <i>n.</i> brightly coloured lizard common in, and exclusive to, the Nelson Province. [<i>Naultinus stellatus</i> aka starred gecko]

>  Nelson green gecko (<i>Naultinus stellatus</i>), Station Creek, Nelson Lakes. <i>(2009,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.doc.govt.nz/conservation/native-animals/reptiles-and-frogs/lizards/geckos/facts/ date estimated]

>  Nelson Green Gecko <i>Heteropholis stellatus</i>: [sic] Also known as the star gecko this gecko is restricted to the Nelson province but can show different patterning depending on the area they are from. <i>(2006,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.reptilepark.co.nz/nzlizard.htm#Nelson%20Green%20Gecko]
