### Nelson huntaway
 
 <i>n.</i> a boulder rolled downhill to move stock [musterers' local]

>  <b>ELWELL</b> In mustering times,Ernest used to set some of the huge boulders on the sides of [the cliffs] rolling, and they make quite a roaring noise ... This noise startled the sheep ... which always made upwards.] <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,169</i>)

>  <b>McCASKILL</b> Danger could also be caused by a musterer rolling a large rock to frighten sheep along and save running a dog. [These rocks, started on purpose, were known as `Nelson huntaways' becauseyoung men from that district used to come mustering with young, untrained dogs. When frustrated they would use any method to move a sheep along the beat. <i>(1969,Molesworth,100</i>) [NOTES: [? 1907-14] A.G. Mowat Diary (? or reminiscence)in 1969 McCaskill Molesworth 100]

>  <b>BAKER</b> Nelson huntaway, a stone rolled down a hillside to move stock below instead of sending a dog out. `From the fact that in the early days the Nelson musterers were noted for having few and poor dogs.' <i>(1941,NZ Slang,59</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Brownie sent a boulder hurtling down the face - a `nelson huntaway' <i>(1949,High Country Days,29</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> On the hill, when sheep are sticking far below you, it's sometimes possible to roll a large rock down a steep face and scare them into movement with its crashing and bounding. This is called a`Nelson huntaway,' or just a `Nelson.' <i>(1951,High Country,42</i>)

>  <b>TOMLINSON</b> A lot of young fellows from Nelson used to start off mustering there [at Hillensden Station, Marlborough] and they became known as the Nelson College. If anyone said `I'm going back to the college, you knew where he was going. Nelson men in Marlborough and North Canterbury used to be called Sandy Hookers. Stones being rolled down steep faces to frighten sheep were called Nelson Huntaways. <i>(1968,Remembered Trails,128</i>)

>  Nelson huntaway n. stone rolled down a hillside to move stock, used instead of sending a dog out. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),14</i>)

### Nelson plan
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LAY</b> The school had been designed, like every other of its era, according to the Nelson Plan. There was an administration and assembly block near the gates, and five identical, detached, two-storeyed classroom blocks behind it, separated from each other by an expanse of concrete. <i>(1988,Fools on the Hill, The,159</i>)

### Nelson scallop
 
 <i>n.</i> (Marine)

>  And then there's Nelson scallops and then asparagus pops out of the ground. <i>(Sep 3, 2006,Sunday Star Times,3</i>)

### Nelson school system
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It is the introduction of what is called the Nelson school system, having been originated by a Presbyterian minister settled in Nelson. <i>(Aug 4, 1911,Maoriland Worker,3</i>)

### Nelson snob
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>THOMSON</b> ...it is worthy of notice, that the colonists, soon after the formation of all the settlements, acquired distinguishing epithets; thus there was an Auckland cove, a Wellington swell, a Nelson snob, a Taranaki exquisite, an Otago cockney, and a Canterbury pilgrim. These epithets, almostalready forgotten, are too characteristic to be buried in oblivion, as each originated in some peculiarity of the leading colonists. The Otago settlers derived theirs from their ignorance of the country in which they lived; the Auckland people from their extensive Sydney connexions; and the origin of the other names are sufficiently obvious without explanation. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,2/188</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Nelson snob a person from Nelson. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,75</i>)

### Nelson system
 
 <i>n.</i> a system of religious instruction for State schools

>  The agreement was made through the supreme courts of the Churches which give the major attention to the Nelson System. <i>(May 3, 1955,Dominion,14</i>)

>  <b>SALMOND</b> Lesson Outlines for Christian Education in New Zealand Schools under the Nelson System [title] <i>(1930,Lesson Outlines for Christian Education</i>)

>  <b>PINFOLD</b> I used to attend, week by week, the State school of the town in which I lived, and gave Bible lessons to the children whose parents indicated their desire for them to remain. This is called the `Nelson System' because it was started in that city. <i>(1930,Fifty Years In Maoriland,133</i>)

>  <b>McSWEENEY</b> When in 1897 the `Nelson system' of religious instruction was introduced by the Rev. J.H.McKenzie, Presbyterian minister at Nelson, Catholic priests followed this method. <i>(1966,History of Catholic Education in N.Z.,208</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Since schools taught for more than the four hours daily required by law, it had become usual, and was legal under the system started in Nelson, for ministers of religion with the approval ofheadmasters and school committees to take classes for half-an-hour weekly, though the children did not have to attend. <i>(1986,Home Front,1/129</i>)

### Nelson weather
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>EARP</b> In other settlements of New Zealand, it is not unusual, in extraordinarily fine weather, tohear the observation - `This is Nelson weather,' ... <i>(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles),143</i>)

### Nelson/Nelson huntaway
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a rock sent down a hillside to disturb sheep [ to save running out a dog.] (See [Taranaki huntaway](../T#taranaki-huntaway),[Maori huntaway](../M#maori-huntaway))

>  <b>MCCASKILL</b> These rocks, started on purpose, were known as 'Nelson huntaways' because young men from that district used to come mustering with young, untrained dogs. <i>(1969,Molesworth,100</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Also reported is Nelson huntaway, a stone rolled down a hillside to move stock below instead of sending a dog out. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,59</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> On the hill, when sheep are sticking far below you, it's sometimes possible to roll a large rock down a steep face and scare them into movement with its crashing and bounding. This is called a "Nelson huntaway" or just a "Nelson." <i>(1951,NZ High Country,42</i>)

>  You only run a 'Nelson huntaway' when the sheep have defied your dogs and they, in turn, have defied you. "If your best dog's tired and beaten early, all you've got left is heart strain, hoarse voice and a Nelson huntaway." [Nelson] <i>(Nov 15, 1983,NZ Journal of Agriculture,20</i>)

### Nelsonian
 
 <i>n.</i> a New Zealand Company sponsored emigrant, Nelson-bound

>  <b>T.J. THOMPSON</b> I think that this question [of a Panama route] will be taken up by the Nelsonians, anxious as most of us are to revisit England <i>(Jan 22, 1842,Diary</i>) [NOTES: on board ship>1842 Jan 22 DIARY [] in 1954 Dec11 Nelson Evening Mail]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A Nelsonian became quite excited theother week and informed us all Nelsonwould be holding holiday that day. Shesaid they called it "Spud Monday" in thegarden province because it was the firstday of spring and the new potatoes wereto be planted that week. <i>(Sep 14, 1927,NZ Tablet,58</i>)

>  As the 5000-odd Golden Bay residents know, the definition of a Nelsonian is someone who was heading for the bay but never quite made it (and thank goodness for that, they might say). <i>(Jan 29, 2001,Evening Post,17</i>)

>  These are the kinds of [sunshine hours] statistics Nelsonians don't just read about; they're statistics we live. <i>(Jun 22, 2008,Sunday  Star Times: Escape,6</i>)

>  .. Nelsonian <i>(1850,Stafford Papers,44,1843 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The Transalpine Nelsonian. <i>(1851,Letter,13</i>) [NOTES: 1851 EJ Wakefield Letter to Sir George Grey 13]

>  <b>MARKS</b> ... the Nelsonians gave us all the encouragement and cheer they could. <i>(1934,Memories (Mainly Merry),35</i>)

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> Everyone knows he was a Nelsonian and took his title from that town, ... <i>(1951,Canterbury Tales,95</i>)

>  <b>MUIR</b> `Good old Nelsonians. Can't miss a chance to plug the sunshine, can you?' <i>(1960,Word for Word,114</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> The religion of many Nelsonians seems to have been of a simple, non-controversial character, `plain but good and improving in its applications'. <i>(1958,Early Victorian NZ,180</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> Behind him, Steven could hear subdued whisperings from a surprising number of curious Nelsonians. <i>(1980,Murder and Chips,5</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Oh, the ruby ripe gooseberries of Christchurch, the shelling of green peas every true Nelsonian loves, ... <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,82</i>)

### Nelsonic
 
 <i>adj.</i> [[?nonce]]

>  <b>CRAWFORD</b> There is a Nelsonic absence of wind [in Havelock]. <i>(1880,Travels in NZ,262</i>)

### Nelsonite
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Oh, you're from Sleepy Hollow...Nelsonites rise to the bait. <i>(Nov 15, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,I/6 : 134</i>)

### Nelsons
 
 <i>n.</i> The Nelson Regiment

>  <b>WAITE</b> Thirty men of the Nelsons were at once doubled over to assist the Indians. <i>(1919,New Zealanders at Gallipoli,54</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [neinei](../N#neinei))

### neo-nutterism
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  "This business of being the saviour of the Pacific Forum is not neo-colonialism. It's neo-nutterism." <i>(Sep 6, 2003,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

### neotribe
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Rata introduced her concept that today's cultural divisiveness results from the rise of the "neotribe", a political organisation fundamentatlly different from the traditional tribe .. evolved from treaty settlements which enable them to privatise and capatalise resourses, and build not just resourses. <i>(Feb 20, 2005,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

### nephrite
 
 <i>n.</i> [for pounamu, greenstone, 'nephrite jade']

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> In Phillip's Mineralogy this stone [pounamu] is described under the name of Nephrite, .. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,38</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> They did not possess implements of greenstone (Nephrite). <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,430</i>)

>  It is called pounamu or poenamu by the Maoris, and `jade,' `jadeite,' or `nephrite,' by various writers, while old books refer to the `green talc' of the Maoris. <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/481</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The Maori greenstone (pounamu) called jade, jadeite, nephrite, etc., by Europeans, is a mineral very restricted in its deposits. ... one variety (tangiwai) is found at Milford Sound. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,248</i>)

>  Pounamu (Nephrite, or Greenstone). - Used for ornamnental purposes. <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/36</i>)

>  <b>ELDER</b> Ninety-nine per cent. of genuine tikis are made of nephrite,the lighter and more translucent varieties of the stone beingpreferred. <i>(1932,Letters & Journals of Samuel Marsden,432,1932 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> Doubtless many of the nephrite implements found have been concealed in former times ... <i>(1937,Moa-Hunters of NZ,205</i>) [NOTES: fn quoting Best Stone Implements of the Maori 172]

>  The Natives are very fond of New-Zeeland green stones [ed. fn nephrite], of mother of Pearl-Shells ... <i>(1982,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,IV/608</i>) [NOTES: 1982 note in 1774 Aug 17 Forster Resolution Journal (1982) IV/608]

>  <i>(Oct 4, 1864,Lyttelton Times,2</i>) [NOTES: <i>Nephrite</i> (Greenstone of the Maoris), in rolled pieces, on teh beach of the West coast.]

>  Besides these there is heitiki, or nephrite neck ornament, and a carved tahaia, or staff of manuka. <i>(Aug 24, 1880,Colonist, The,2</i>)

### Neptune's necklace
 
 <i>n.</i> = Venus' necklace

>  <b>CROWE</b> Neptune's necklace <i>Hormosira banksii</i>... A well-known seaweed up to 50 cm high, made up of pimply olive-brown hollow beads, each usually about 1 cm across. <i>(1997,Field Guide to Native Edible Plants of NZ,149</i>)

>  <b>CROWE</b> Neptune's necklace <i>Hormosira banksii</i>... A well-known seaweed up to 50 cm high, made up of pimply olive-brown hollow beads, each usually about 1 cm across. <i>(1997,Field Guide to Native Edible Plants of NZ,149</i>)

>  <b>CROWE</b> Neptune's necklace <i>Hormosira banksii</i>... A well-known seaweed up to 50 cm high, made up of pimply olive-brown hollow beads, each usually about 1 cm across. <i>(1997,Field Guide to Native Edible Plants of NZ,149</i>)

>  <b>CROWE</b> Neptune's necklace <i>Hormosira banksii</i>... A well-known seaweed up to 50 cm high, made up of pimply olive-brown hollow beads, each usually about 1 cm across. <i>(1997,Field Guide to Native Edible Plants of NZ,149</i>)

>  <b>CROWE</b> Neptune's necklace <i>Hormosira banksii</i>... A well-known seaweed up to 50 cm high, made up of pimply olive-brown hollow beads, each usually about 1 cm across. <i>(1997,Field Guide to Native Edible Plants of NZ,149</i>)

>  <b>CROWE</b> Neptune's necklace <i>Hormosira banksii</i>... A well-known seaweed up to 50 cm high, made up of pimply olive-brown hollow beads, each usually about 1 cm across. <i>Ibid.</i> 150 When the incidence of goitre was high in New Zealand, a schoolteacher here claimed to have appreciably reduced the goitre incidence in his school by getting the children to eat one 'bead' of Neptune's necklace a day. <i>(1997,Field Guide to Native Edible Plants of NZ,149</i>)

>  <b>HULME & MORRISON</b> Neptune's necklace (again, just 'rimu') is tangy when you nibble it, and so are the young tips of rimuroa (bladderweed). <i>(1989,Homeplaces,61</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Fig.1. Seaweeds of the intertidal belt, Stewart Island, here consisting mainly of Bull-kelp (<i>Durvillea</i>), Strap-weed (<i> Xiphophora</i>), Moss-weed (<i> Bostrychia</i>), Neptune's Necklace (<i> Hormosira</i>), and <i> Sea Bombs</i> [sic] (<i> Adenocystis</i>). <i>(1961,Flora NZ,4,1929 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DEMPSEY</b> Every shallow pool is a garden of seaweed, with long ruffles of sea lettuce, carrageen and Neptune's necklace. <i>(1964,Little World of Stewart island,25</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Hansard ... could see [in Otago Harbour] large chitons and delicate seaweed overhung with beads of a coral-like growth - perhaps the seaweed that children call `Neptune's necklace'. <i>(1978,Cruise of the Acheron,109</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Other edible varieties of seaweed commomly used by the Maori include rimurapu or bull kelp (Durvillea antarctica), which was also dried and then boiled with tutu juice to form a jelly, and the beaded Neptune's <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,9</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i> Hormosira banksii</i> NEPTUNE'S NECKLACE ... Venus' necklace ... Necklace seaweed ... Brown or yellow-brown strings of thick-walled bladders, flopped over rocks ... at low tide. necklace (<i> Hormosira banksii</i>), which is palatable raw. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,318</i>)

### nest of gold
 
 <i>n.</i> see cit.

>  Another piece showed a nest of gold about an inch square. This stone is still more closely impregnated with gold. <i>(Nov 21, 1867,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  It is very common in quartz-specimens to get what is called a nest of gold, which generally consists of one large speck surrounded by a number of smaller ones at various distances from it. <i>(1890,TrNZI,22/401</i>)

### nester
 
 <i>n.</i> a dog trained to find rabbits' nests



 
 <i>n.</i> a baby rabbit, still in the breeding nest [farming]

>  <b>McCASKILL</b> Rabbiters ... would receive a bonus for 1s. on each large skin and 4d. on each nester <i>(1969,Molesworth,160</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a dog that is skilled at uncovering rabbit breeding nests. [rabbiting]

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> nester: a dog in the rabbiter's pack skilled in finding the rabbits' breeding nests. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,55</i>)

>  Nester: Dog for finding rabbits' breeding nests. Most rabbiters have one in their pack. (C.) <i>(Nov 11, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  nester: (?obs) a dog for finding rabbits' nests or breeding places. <i>(1980,Goldie Brown Collection</i>)

### nesting mussel
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Most of the others [sc. mussels] - flea mussels, mud mussels, date mussels and nesting mussels - are too small to be worthy of the pot. <i>(Feb 19, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),58</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> NESTING MUSSEL (<i>Ryenella impacta</i>) gets its name from the fact that it `spins' a nest of byssus thread and lives communally in a large matt-like group. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,169</i>)

### nestor
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HAY</b> Pigeons, nestors, parrots, rail, kiwi, swamp-fowl, water-fowl, owls, parson-birds, all these and more were eaten; while the native dogs and rats were held to be great dainties. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/151</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> kaka

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Before it was quite dark, flights of the Austral Nestor passed over our encampment, shrieking in a dismal manner, and alighted for a moment on one of the dead trees ... <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/151</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a fungus whose mature form is of a ball-shaped net or lattice, also called basket-fungus, fairy's closet (See [fungus](../F#fungus))

### net-gun
 
 <i>n.</i> [deerhunters]

>  But may I suggest that in a future edition [of the Collins English Dictionary two more New Zealand words which are associated with deer hunting be included, namely net -gun', and the verb `to bulldog'. <i>(Apr 5, 1987,Dunedin Star Weekender,16</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a capture gun, incorporating a net, used for live deer capture.

>  <b>FORRESTER</b> .. the method was superseded by the net-gun. Net-guns made on old .303 Lee Enfield actions would mostly blow up or disintegrate with constant firing ... <i>(1983,Chopper Boys, The,63</i>)

>  <b>FORRESTER</b> .. around ten helicopters shot themselves down in a very short space of time with these hand-held net-guns. <i>(1983,Chopper Boys, The,65</i>)

### nettle
 
 <i>n.</i> native nettles <i>Urtica</i> spp. esp. <i>U. ferox (?= U. incisa) </i> (See [ongaonga](../O#ongaonga))

>  Amongst the known kinds of plants are common and rough Bindweed, [ed. fn 4 Probably <i>Calystegia sepium</i> and the smaller <i>C. turguriorum</i>.] Nightshade [author's fnd <i>Solanum Linnaei</i>. [ed. note [Probably <i>Solanum aviculare</i>, described by Forster from Queen Charlotte Sound: ...]]] and Nettles, [fne Urtica [ed. [<i>Urtica ferox</i>]]] both which grow to the size of small trees; a shruby [sic] Speedwell ... <i>(1777,Cook's Journals,3/804-805</i>) [NOTES: Queen Charlotte Sound1777 Feb Anderson's Journal in Cook Journals (1967)]

>  The gardens [of Totoe's village at the mouth of the Mataura] were kept very clear from weeds; Nettles grow in abundance, and it is said that this alone is [63] a proof of the goodness of theland. <i>(1986,Boultbee Journal,62,1986 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> The New Zealand Nettles are similar in general appearance to the English, but quite different specifically. As a genus <i>Urtica</i> is known by its small unisexual flowers ... <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 224</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Nettle. <i>Urtica</i>, various sp. <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  <i>Urtica ferox</i> nettle <i>(1896,Dunedin Field Club Plant Catalogues</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> tree(-)nettle (see also nettle-tree) [cf and stinging tree 1827] x-ref only now at tree-nettle]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>Urtica ferox</i> tree-nettle <i>(1906,NZ Flora</i>) [NOTES: And.]

>  <b>C-6</b> <i>Urtica ferox</i> tree-nettle <i>(1915,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: Notes on the Plant Covering of Kennedy's Bush ... CanterburyAnd]

>  <i>nettle, tree [s] Urtica ferox </i> Forst.f. <i>Ibid.</i> 80 <i>tree nettle [s] Urtica ferox</i> Forst.f. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,52</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>URTICA FEROX, ongaonga</i>. The tree nettle can form great thickets taller than a man. Pale stinging hairs on leaves and stems are poisonous, a real danger to horses and dogs. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,82</i>)

>  It is possible to proceed ..., but beware of the native tree nettle, ongaonga! Here [at Nugget Point, South Otago] is the habitat of the world's rarest penguin: the yellow-eyed penguin. <i>(1988,Pacific Way,48</i>)

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> stingless nettle

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>Urtica australis</i> subantarctic nettle <i>(1909,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: Report on a Botanical Survey of Stewart Island]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>nettle, tree [s] Urtica ferox </i> Forst.f. true nettle [?error for TREE-]

>  2. various introduced nettles, esp. of <i>Urtica</i> spp. see also DEAD NETTLE, RED DEAD NETTLE a.<i> U. urens</i>, (ANNUAL) NETTLE <i>(1907,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: Cockayne Report on a Botanical Survey of Kapiti Island (And)]

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Urtica ferox</i>, =NETTLE(-TREE) (?growing like a bush; ?in the or at the edge of the BUSH A1)

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Urtica linearifolia</i>

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

### nettle-leaved
 
 <i>adj.</i> (See [fat hen](../F#fat-hen),[goosefoot](../G#goosefoot))

### nettle-tree
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Urtica ferox</i> ongaonga, tree-nettle

>  <b>R TAYLOR</b> The nettle-tree, a singular but disagreeable shrub attains a height of about twelve feet, its long, narrow leaf is covered with large white spines very sharp and painful if touched <i>(1846,Journal,IV/117</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> We passed by a nettle tree which the old chief [100] immediately cut down as being a very disagreeable thing for naked feet to come near. <i>(May 25, 1849,Journal,6/99</i>)

>  <i>Urtica ferox</i>. nettle tree HMB, 1898. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1</i>)

>  Nettle tree. Urtica ferox, Forst. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/48</i>) [NOTES: Buchanan Botany Otago]

>  <i>nettle-tree</i> n. <i>several trees the leaves of which possess rigid stinging hairs. </i> <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,8</i>)

>  <i>nettle-tree</i> n. large scrub tree with broad leaves and virulent stinging hairs. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),621</i>)

>  <i>nettle-tree</i> large scrub tree with broad leaves and virulent stinging hairs. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),14</i>)

>  <b>BAYLY</b> All the trees keep a continual verdure all the year. I saw nettle (?)[McNab's query] trees here of 9 and 10 inches round. <i>(Apr 12, 1773,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1773 April 12 Bayly's Journal in 1908 McNabHistorical Records 2/206 [?ghost: McNab is doubtful of the ?form or ?meaning; are there nettle trees so-called in e.g. the West Indies that Bayly might be thinking of?]]

### netty
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LYNN</b> The old Privy ... WC ... Lavatory ... Dunney ... Netty ... Little House ... Little House at the Bottom of the Garden ... or any one of the countless names it was called, ... - <i>(1982,Under the Lynnwood Tree,125</i>)

### neuter-athon
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Activities, such as the neuter-athon, resulted in complaints of professional misconduct to the Veterinary Council (they were not upheld). <i>(Feb 2005,North & South,68</i>)

>  During the annual week long neuter-athon to reduce the wild cat population Ward-Mcgrath does about 600 free castrations. The tom with the biggest testicles wins two months' supply of cat food. <i>(Feb 2005,North & South,68</i>)

### never-never
 
 <i>n.</i> [never-nevers, never never, never nevers]

>  A shearer comes along with a cheque for 80 or 100 [pounds], gives it to the publican, gets rapidly drunk, and then drunker, and then D.T.'s and then wakes up and goes back into the never-never to rake up another cheque. <i>(Oct 3, 1908,NZ Truth,172/4/5</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> To have lived such an open life and then to be suddenly caged up in an enclosure such as this Army camp often tempted me to make a break for the never-never, ... <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,108</i>)

>  never-never n. the thinly populated country far from settlement, esp. in Western Queensland and the Northern Territory. [prob. fr. Abor. (Comderio) nie-vah vahs, unoccupied land] <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),621</i>)

>  Never Never n. the sparsely populated far outback, the back-blocks. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),14</i>)

>  never-never ... NZ & Austral. any remote area, esp. land beyond backblocks in central Australia. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,505</i>) [NOTES: cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 758; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 327]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The other four shooters were out on the job for a few more days fly-camping, so the two of us had the first night in the never-nevers to ourselves, ... <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,32</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

### new
 
 <i>adj.</i> >in various uses in NZ relating to Pakeha immigration and settlement [cf AND New 1. with nouns denoting land, country, settlement not previously occupied by white people 1835 2. with nouns denoting recently arrived new settler

### New  Zealand violet
 
 <i>n.</i>

### new bell
 
 <i>n.</i> new boy [ca1943 Wellington Boys' College (H.S. Gajadhar)]

### new bug
 
 <i>n.</i> new boy [1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 25 NEW BUGS -->1951 16-18 M 26 Marlborough C. 25 NEW BUGS (5) -->1960 16C F B1 Nelson Girls C. 25 NEW-BUGS -->1951 14-16 M 59 Wellington H.S. 25 NEW BUGS]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> But curiously enough, someone considered [at Nelson Boys' College] `a dag', `a bit of a one', `a rough diamond', could get away with a lot, even a lowly new bug; ... [c1930s] <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,148</i>)

### new chum
 
 <i>n.</i> a newly arrived settler or immigrant; a novice [See 1871 citation]

>  "Aw, may I awsk what a colonial is?" said a supercillious new chum the other day to a full-blooded Aucklander in Queen-st. "Certainly, a colonial is an Englishman," was the answer, "with all the modern improvements." <i>(Apr 2, 1887,Te Aroha News,2</i>)

>  New chums come and go in great numbers. <i>(Nov 14, 1855,Nelson Examiner,2</i>)

>  They naturally think they are equally as worthy attention as new chums. <i>(Apr 12, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,6</i>)

>  Mr. Singleton is a recent arrival in the colony - a new chum, so to speak. <i>(Oct 4, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,19</i>)

>  Our driver.. was a bit of a "new chum" at the wheel. <i>(Apr 1, 1924,Otago Witness,72</i>)

>  SIR,- Some numbers of your paper, containing an effusion of two from a new chum, y'clept "A Pilgrim of the Plains," have just reached this out-of-the-way place. <i>(Feb 4, 1854,Lyttelton Times,7</i>)

>  One of the party (our new chum, who would wear a coat) not being used to river wading got giddy when he had reached the middle, and decalred he should fall, and could not go a step further; when losing both his nerve and balance at the same time, he was carried off his feet and floated down the river. <i>(Jan 20, 1863,Colonist (Nelson),3</i>)

>  "Well I don't wonder at a new chum like you, fresh from the drawing rooms of Belgravia, being surprised at anything you see out here," replied Harold Courtney, in a tone of veiled sarcasm; "but if you knew the circumstances you would understand that his marrying a Maori wife and settling down at Stratford was the easiest and most natural thing in the world." <i>(Dec 15, 1896,Observer (Auckland),17</i>)

>  As he is, comparatively, a new chum, he clearly was not aware of the conditions when the bridge was built. <i>(Jul 23, 1904,Wanganui Chronicle,2</i>)

>  I am - a New Chum. My business is to critcise, moralise upon, compare, sketch, and, where necessary, correct or praise the peculiarities of my adopted countrymen. First, then, arises this question: what is a 'New Chum'? and it may be answered in two ways, according to the manner in which it is asked. For there is a real New Chum; and there is a New Chum that is ideal only. The New Chum proper, is, I understand, a person who has but freshly come to the Colony from some part or other of the old world. The New chum that most colonists, here, appear to have in their mind's eye, is quite a different being in most cases. He is a pattern of neatness and elegance in his dress; of cheek and superciliousness in his deportment; of ignorance, meanness, cowardice, spiritlessness, vagueness, helplessness in time of emergency, vanity, presumption, and emptiness, in his temperament; and of all these vices, combined with effeminate and pedantic precision, in his talk. His delight is to abuse colonial customs; to run down the local 'bigwigs'; to show contempt for the industries and pursuits of the colonists generally; and affect a languid indifference to the matters which most engage their interest. His purse is even emptier than his head. He is without resources and without pluck. His ambition is at once low and unreasonable. he puts his trust in letters of introduction, and looks for a Government billet. <i>(Aug 30, 1871,Timaru Herald,6</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> ... the sooner a `New Chum' loses his identity as such, the better for himself. <i>(1913,Country Work,5</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an initiate, or one inexperienced in farming.

>  To their credit .. farmers out here are ever willing to teach a 'new chum ... ' <i>(Aug 27, 1864,Weekly News,7</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> A stranger, or colonially speaking, a 'new chum' would have thought we must needs cross that barrier-range before we could penetrate any distance into the back-country ... <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,63</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> If an unfortunate new chum ventures mildly to protest against some trifling hardships, he is at once silenced by being told that it is evident that he is not 'colonised' yet and that he must get used to such things. <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,8</i>)

>  There are of course, new-chums and new-chums; we speak of those who come out to the colony burning with British eagerness to own land and cultivate it. <i>(Aug 1882,Northern New Zealand Settler,3</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> A new chum who was working at the station had a theory that a beast would not charge if one turned his back on it.. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,137</i>)

>  <b>MULGAN</b> In New Zealand the joke is rather on the 'new chum' who shows his ignorance of country life like the Bulletin's English visitor to the sheep station who shoots his host's prize ram in the belief that it is a wild animal. <i>(1946,Pastoral NZ,94</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Altogether the neighbours, so critical of 'new chums' formed a high opinion of him. <i>(1955,Land of My Children,170</i>)

>  If he succeeds in satisfying his companion that he is fit to be trusted alone, our new chum will presently be sent off on a sweep of this own ... At this preliminary stage of a muster men often work in pairs, and the new chum will be sent with an old hand. <i>(Apr 13, 1955,Weekly News,34</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> They [Merinos] roamed far in the days before fences, and mustering was difficult. The new chum found to his cost that 'they'd rush for miles at the sound of a bark, and you would have a pile up and a smother on your hands.' <i>(1976,From the Beginning: chronicles of a county,131</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> His [harvesting] fork was his own and tapu'd an inch thick. Lord help any new chum who had the impudence to touch it. [newch'm] <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,41</i>)

### new chum dam
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [swinging rafter dam](../S#swinging-rafter-dam))

### new chum gold
 
 <i>n.</i> [also new chums' gold]

>  <b>COOPER</b> Many new comers are misled by the abundance of mica and mundie [sic], which is consequently called `new chums' gold', and it is difficult to convince them that they have not got theprecious metal. <i>(Nov 12, 1867,Digger's Diary,12,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAULIN</b> The sand [in Dusky Sound] ... glittered with scales of yellow mica. It is sometimes called New Chum Gold, as, seeing it for the first time, one can hardly believe it is not the precious metal itself. <i>(1889,Wild West Coast,14</i>)

>  When the battered dish flowed empty, bar a tail of new-chum gold,/ Or it gave a ring of `colour' that betokened wealth untold. <i>(1985,Penguin Book of NZ Verse,93</i>) [NOTES: c1890-1900 EDWIN EDWARDS `Waitekauri Every Time' in 1985 Penguin Book of NZ Verse 93]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> We found traces of gold at different places, golden mica, known as new chum gold, and two types of pyrites. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,73</i>)

### new chummy
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  I think the price asked depends in a great measure upon the appearance of the purchaser - those with a `new chummy' look paying through the nose ... <i>(Jan 18, 1862,Otago Witness</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Dec 16 C.M.O. to Lyttelton Times in 1862 Jan 18 Otago Witness (Griffiths Collection)]

### new chumship
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BUTLER</b> ... I was anxious to become an old chum, as colonial dialect calls a settler - thereby proving my new chumship most satisfactorily. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,iv : 55</i>)
